\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status (manuscript 05).} Part~III is an AI-assisted, unrefereed
research manuscript. Its arguments are conventional mathematical proofs, not
Lean or Rocq formalizations, and nothing in it is formalized in ProveIt. The
contribution it proposes as new is the filter-profile classification, that
is, the identification of the finite-output invariant with local filter
capacities, and its consequences: the capacity-attainment criterion, the
three-color separation and the ultrafilter threshold laws. It makes no
separate priority claim for the elementary supersequence/feedback-set
reduction (\cref{fp:lem:word-fvs}), the random-order bound
(\cref{fp:lem:random}) or the balanced-clique arc extremum
(\cref{fp:thm:turan}); its literature search was targeted, not exhaustive,
and does not certify priority. The classification concerns a finite
nonempty derived set: it does not settle finite heights $h\ge2$, infinite
derived sets, transfinite play or expected-cost optimization, it makes no
claim about arbitrary ordinal-length play or the point-separating invariant
of \cite{CCS}, and it does not claim Part~II's Research question~2 on
minimum synchronization families (its ultrafilter thresholds count local
components while permitting every label at finitely many isolated
exceptions, a specific input model). It does not refute Part~II's upper
bound, which remains valid. Free ultrafilters are used in ZFC; their
existence is not certified by the finite computations, which never construct
or simulate an infinite ultrafilter. The only imported complexity-theoretic
fact is the NP-completeness of directed feedback vertex set \cite{GMM}; the
reductions to layer count and to rounded depth are proved, for an explicit
finite profile encoding only.
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{The manuscript's title block and abstract}
\label{fp:sec:abstract}
\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Filter Profiles and Open-Query Complexity. Exact finite-derivative
classification, ultrafilter thresholds, and a three-color separation.}
Research report prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov, September 29, 2026.

An open-query strategy identifies the label of a hidden point by adaptively
asking membership in open sets. We continue the finite-output open-query
program in ProveIt, concentrating on its question about the local hypotheses
needed for sharp Cantor--Bendixson bounds. For every Hausdorff space with a
finite nonempty derived set, we describe the complete word profile of every
finite coloring by a finite directed graph. The minimum layer count is the
number of used colors plus the graph's minimum directed feedback vertex
number; the minimum query depth is its binary logarithm rounded upward.

The worst-case invariant depends only on finite partition capacities of the
neighborhood filters at the nonisolated points. A precise bin-partition
criterion characterizes attainment of the rank-one layer bound. For three
colors it yields a complete numerical classification. In particular, three
one-point free-ultrafilter spaces require at most two questions, whereas
three convergent sequences can require three, although their
Cantor--Bendixson derivative cardinalities agree. For sums of arbitrary numbers
of ultrafilter components, an exact directed extremal calculation gives every
alphabet-dependent threshold, with a characterization of the graphs attaining
the minimum arc count. Full proofs, constructive strategy certificates,
finite exhaustive checks, and a further research program are included.

\textbf{Status and scope.}
This is an AI-assisted, unrefereed research manuscript. The arguments below
are conventional mathematical proofs, not Lean or Rocq formalizations.
The new contribution proposed here is the filter-profile classification and
its consequences for the repository's finite-output question. We make no
separate priority claim for the elementary supersequence/feedback-set
reduction or the extremal graph ingredients, and do not claim a solution of
all finite-height or transfinite open-query problems. Free ultrafilters are
used in ZFC; their existence is not certified by the finite computations.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Notation of Part~III}
\label{fp:sec:notation}
Part~III fixes a Hausdorff space $X$ with a finite nonempty derived set
$X'=\{p_1,\ldots,p_t\}$ and a coloring $c:X\to[q]=\{1,\ldots,q\}$, Part~II's
alphabet $\Sigma$ of $q$ letters. Several letters are used differently in
Part~III and in Parts~I--II, and two carry two meanings inside Part~III. Where
a false reading is tempting, the table prints it beside the true one.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.13\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.44\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Part~III & Parts~I--II\\
\midrule
\endhead
$\sm_q$ & $\sm_q(X)=\sup_c\depth(X,c)$ over $c:X\to[q]$; colorings need not
use every label. The manuscript's macro, printed identically. & Part~II's
$\smq(X)$, the same invariant; Part~I's $\sm(X)$ is an ordinal, equal to
$\operatorname{sm}_2(X)$ when either is finite.\\
$\layers$, $\depth$ & Least number of layers and least worst-case depth,
printed $\mathsf L$ and $\mathsf d$ in the manuscript. In
\cref{fp:prop:complexity}, $\depth(G')$ is the depth of a space realizing the
profile graph $G'$. & Part~II's $\layers(X,c)$, $\depth(X,c)$; Part~I's
$L(A,X)$.\\
$h$, ``rank one'' & Part~III settles the class $0<\lvert X'\rvert<\infty$,
where $h=1$ and the rank-one bound is $(q-1)\cdot1+s=q+s-1$. \emph{False:}
``Part~III settles every space with $h=1$''; $[0,\omega^2)$ has $h=1$ and an
infinite derived set (\cref{fp:q:infinite}). & Last derivative rank.\\
$t$ & $t=\lvert X'\rvert$, the number of nonisolated points. & Part~II: the
number of towers; $\bigsqcup_{i=1}^t\Tw_2$ has $\lvert X'\rvert=t$, so the
meanings agree.\\
$D_i$, $D$, $N_i$, $Z$ & $X=Z\sq N_1\sq\cdots\sq N_t$ with clopen
$N_i\ni p_i$; $D_i=N_i\setminus\{p_i\}$ its isolated part; $D$ an infinite
set carrying a filter. \emph{Not} the depth $\depth$. & Part~I:
$D_j=X^{(j)}\setminus X^{(j+1)}$ and the oriented lengths $N_0,N_1$; Part~II:
$N$ an input length.\\
$\F$, $\I_\F$, $\U$, $S(\F)$ & A free filter, its dual ideal, a free
ultrafilter, and the one-point space on $D\cup\{p\}$ whose neighborhoods of
$p$ are $\{p\}\cup A$, $A\in\F$. For the cofinite filter on a countable $D$,
$S(\F)\cong[0,\omega]=\omega+1$. & Part~I: $S=[0,\omega]$; Part~II:
$S\subseteq\Sigma$ a set of first letters.\\
$b_i$, $b(\F)$, $\boldsymbol b$ & The partition capacity at $p_i$, in
$\N_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}$, and the list of capacities. \emph{Not} Part~II's
coloring $b_{a,m}$. & $b_{a,m}$ a coloring of $\Tw_m$
(\cref{fo:sec:CB}).\\
$B_i$, $B$, $\B$ & The sets of colors whose cells are positive at $p_i$;
$\B=\PP(D)/\I_\F$ a Boolean algebra. & ---\\
$s$, $S$ & In \eqref{fp:eq:active-centers} and \cref{fp:thm:attainment}: the
number of colors on $X'$ and their set, as in Part~II's \cref{fo:thm:CB}. But
in $T(q,r)$ and \cref{fp:thm:turan}, $q=ar+s$ with $0\le s<r$: $s$ is a
\emph{remainder}. \emph{False:} reading the $s$ of $T(q,r)$ as a number of
colors. & Part~II: $s=\lvert S\rvert$, the number of colors on
$X^{(h)}$.\\
$a$, $a_i$ & $a_i=c(p_i)$, the center color at $p_i$; but in $q=ar+s$, $a$ is
a quotient. \emph{False:} the $a$ of $T(q,r)$ is a color. & Part~II: $a\in\Sigma$
a letter.\\
$q_c$, $\Sigma_c$ & The number and the set of colors $c$ uses. & ---\\
$G_c$, $\tau$, $\ac$, $\ell$ & The profile digraph on $\Sigma_c$; the minimum
directed feedback vertex number; the largest acyclic induced subgraph; the
shortest word containing every vertex and arc as subsequences. & Part~I:
$G_i$ open sets.\\
$r$, $r_*$, $r_q$, $T$ & A bound on $\ac$ (the number of cliques of an
extremal graph); $r_*(q,t)$ and $r_q$ of \eqref{fp:eq:ultra-intro} and
\cref{fp:cor:jump}; $T(q,r)$ the least number of arcs with $\ac\le r$. &
Part~II: $r$ the number of words or chains.\\
$k$, $P$ & $k=\ceil{\log_2q}$ in \cref{fp:cor:jump}; a feedback budget in
\cref{fp:prop:complexity} and the size of a feedback set in
\cref{fp:lem:word-fvs}; $P$ a power of two in \cref{fp:prop:complexity}. &
Part~I: $K_k$; Part~II: $P$ a poset.\\
$X_t$ & The sum of $t$ one-point free-ultrafilter spaces
(\cref{fp:sec:ultra}). & Part~I: $X_{h,m}=[0,\omega^h\cdot m]$.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{Parts~I and~II inside Part~III}
\label{fp:sec:crosswalk}
The following checks were made when Part~III was added; they are
consequences of the three parts' proofs, not new theorems.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Two labels.} For $q=2$ every capacity list is realizable with
both arcs between the two labels as soon as $t\ge2$, and with at most one arc
when $t=1$; so \cref{fp:thm:capacity} gives $\operatorname{sm}_2(X)=1$ for
$t=1$ and $2$ for $t\ge2$, whatever the capacities. This is Part~I's
\cref{thm:height} at $h=1$ ($\ceil{\log_22}=1$, $\ceil{\log_23}=2$) for
spaces with a finite nonempty derived set. The manuscript states it for
ultrafilter sums in \cref{fp:sec:ultra}.
\item \emph{The upper bound.} When all $q$ labels are used,
\eqref{fp:eq:active-centers} reads $\layers(X,c)\le q+s-1$, which is Part~II's
\eqref{fo:eq:CB-bound} at $h=1$. \Cref{fp:thm:attainment} decides for every
such space and every $s$ whether it is attained.
\item \emph{Towers.} Part~II's $\Tw_2$ is the convergent sequence
$[0,\omega]$, which is compact and first countable. For $t$ copies,
\cref{fp:cor:regular-formula} gives
$\ceil{\log_2(q-1+\min(q,t))}$, which is \eqref{fo:eq:tower-invariant} at
$m=2$. Part~III extends that value to every space with a finite nonempty
derived set whose nonisolated points are first countable or locally compact,
and shows by \cref{fp:thm:ultra} that it fails for sums of free-ultrafilter
spaces: at $q=t=3$ the tower value is $\ceil{\log_25}=3$, the ultrafilter
value~$2$ \eqref{fp:eq:separation}.
\item \emph{No contradiction.} Part~II's remark after
\cref{fo:thm:tower-invariant} states that \cref{fo:thm:CB} is a uniform bound
over a class and does not assert that every space of the given height has
the same invariant; the README of this report says the same. The separation
\eqref{fp:eq:separation} shows that for $q=3$ they do not. No statement of
Part~I or Part~II is refuted, and none is retracted.
\item \emph{Words.} By \cref{fp:thm:profile}, the words that accumulate at a
point of a finite derived set are the two-letter words $ab$ of the arcs of
$G_c$, and $\ell(G)$ of \cref{fp:lem:word-fvs} is the shortest common
supersequence of these words and of the single letters: the two-letter case
of Part~II's supersequence representation (\cref{fo:thm:chains}).
\item \emph{Complexity.} Part~II's \cref{fo:thm:NP} (fixed $q\ge3$, posets and
tower lists) and \cref{fp:prop:complexity} (unbounded $q$, profile graphs)
concern different input models; see the note after
\cref{fp:prop:complexity}.
\item \emph{Independent checks.} A script written at intake and rerun at this
write (not shipped) confirmed $\ell(G)=\lvert V\rvert+\tau(G)$ on all 4,165
loopless digraphs on one to four labelled vertices by a breadth-first search
over words; $T(q,r)$ and the symmetry of every equality graph for $q\le4$;
on 172 capacity lists over $\{1,2,3,\infty\}$ for $q=2,3,4$, the ternary
formula \eqref{fp:eq:ternary-intro}, the bin criterion against brute-force
star realization, and, for lists of infinite capacities,
\eqref{fo:eq:tower-invariant} at $m=2$; the layer formula of
\cref{fp:thm:ultra} for $q\le4$; and all 63 rows of
\path{data/03-filter-profiles-query_jump_thresholds.csv} against a direct
scan.
\end{enumerate}

\section{The repository question and the main conclusions}
\label{fp:sec:intro}

\subsection{A specific source and a specific question}

The starting point is ProveIt's report \emph{Open-query games on ordinal
spaces}, particularly Part II, \cref{fo:sec:questions}, \Cref{fo:q:invariants}
(Parts~I and~II above). The inspected revision is
\begin{center}
\texttt{2d49198382cc068f46747d1b3d9067d47cee4f24}.
\end{center}
The report asks which local conditions make the finite-output upper bound
\((q-1)h+s\) attainable, and whether accumulating word profiles determine
the invariant. Here \(h\) is the index of the last nonempty
Cantor--Bendixson derivative and \(s\) is the number of colors on that
last derivative. The report explicitly distinguishes this question for
\(q\geq 3\) from its binary classification.

We solve the profile problem for all Hausdorff spaces whose derived set is
finite and nonempty, hence for \(h=1\). We also determine exactly when the
bound is attained in that class. This is a resolution of a substantive
special case of the repository question and a negative answer to the
possible stronger assertion that rank and last-derivative size alone always
suffice for finite-output games. It is not a refutation of the report's
upper bound: that bound remains valid.

\begin{writenotec}[Scope: ``hence for $h=1$'']
The class settled here, Hausdorff spaces whose derived set $X'$ is finite
and nonempty, lies inside the class $h=1$ but is a proper subclass of it: the
ordinal space $[0,\omega^2)$ has the infinite discrete derived set
$\{\omega\cdot n:n\ge1\}$ and empty second derivative, so $h=1$ there too.
Such spaces are the subject of \cref{fp:q:infinite}. Part~II's
\cref{fo:q:invariants} is therefore answered for finite nonempty derived
sets, not for all of $h=1$ (see the note after that question).
\end{writenotec}

The broader cut-and-choose setting comes from Chiozini, Csern\'ak and
Soukup \cite{CCS}. Our invariant is finite-output and finite-depth; no
claim below concerns arbitrary ordinal-length play or the point-separating
invariant of that paper.

\subsection{Overview of the results}

Fix a nonempty Hausdorff space \(X\) with
\[
 X'=\{p_1,\ldots,p_t\},\qquad 1\leq t<\infty.
\]
For a coloring \(c\), write \(q_c=|c[X]|\). There is a canonically determined
local profile at each \(p_i\), after choosing disjoint clopen neighborhoods.
It consists of the color \(a_i=c(p_i)\) and the colors \(B_i\) whose cells
accumulate at \(p_i\). Form a loopless digraph \(G_c\) on the used colors
with arcs
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:graph-intro}
 a_i\longrightarrow b\quad(b\in B_i,\ b\ne a_i).
\end{equation}
If \(\tau(G)\) is the minimum number of vertices whose deletion destroys
all directed cycles, our first exact formula is
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:main-intro}
 \boxed{\quad
 \layers(X,c)=q_c+\tau(G_c),\qquad
 \depth(X,c)=\ceil{\log_2(q_c+\tau(G_c))}.
 \quad}
\end{equation}
The full admissible-word profile, not merely these two minima, is described
in \cref{fp:thm:profile}.

A second invariant \(b_i\in\N_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}\) measures how many
disjoint color cells can simultaneously accumulate at \(p_i\). These
capacities determine the worst case over all \(q\)-colorings. For prescribed
\(s\) colors on \(X'\), the maximum possible layer count equals \(q+s-1\)
if and only if the indices \(1,\ldots,t\) can be partitioned into \(s\)
nonempty classes, each with total capacity at least \(s-1\)
(\cref{fp:thm:attainment}).

For three colors, put
\[
 u=|\{i:b_i=1\}|,\qquad v=|\{i:b_i\geq2\}|.
\]
Then the entire worst-case query invariant has the simple form
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:ternary-intro}
 \boxed{\quad
 \sm_3(X)=
 \begin{cases}
 3,&v+\floor{u/2}\geq3,\\
 2,&v+\floor{u/2}<3.
 \end{cases}\quad}
\end{equation}
Here \(b_i=1\) means exactly that the local neighborhood filter is an
ultrafilter. Thus even the first nontrivial finite-output case detects
local information absent from the derivative cardinalities.

Finally, for a sum \(X_t\) of \(t\) free-ultrafilter components, define
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:T-intro}
 T(q,r)=r a(a-1)+2as,\qquad q=ar+s,\quad 0\leq s<r,
\end{equation}
and
\[
 r_*(q,t)=\min\{r\in\{1,\ldots,q\}:T(q,r)\leq t\}.
\]
The exact worst-case formulas are
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:ultra-intro}
 \boxed{\quad
 \max_{c:X_t\to[q]}\layers(X_t,c)=2q-r_*(q,t),\qquad
 \sm_q(X_t)=\ceil{\log_2(2q-r_*(q,t))}.
 \quad}
\end{equation}
All displayed formulas are proved below, including their boundary cases.

\section{Open questions, layers, and admissible words}
\label{fp:sec:normal}

\begin{definition}
Let \(c:X\to\Sigma\) be a coloring into a finite set. An open-query
strategy is a finite binary decision tree whose internal nodes are labeled
by open subsets of \(X\). A point \(x\) follows the yes branch at a node
labeled \(U\) precisely when \(x\in U\). A strategy is correct if the label
at the reached leaf is \(c(x)\) for every \(x\in X\).
The minimum worst-case depth is \(\depth(X,c)\). We set
\[
 \sm_q(X)=\sup\{\depth(X,c):c:X\to\{1,\ldots,q\}\}.
\]
Colorings need not be continuous. Queries have unit cost, and the objective
is worst-case rather than expected depth.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A layer representation of \(c\) is a finite ordered partition
\(X=E_1\sq\cdots\sq E_m\) into nonempty monochromatic sets such that
every suffix
\[
 U_j=\bigcup_{k\geq j}E_k
\]
is open. Its word is the sequence of colors of its layers. The minimum
number of layers is \(\layers(X,c)\). A word is called admissible if it
supports such a representation with empty positions permitted.
\end{definition}

Allowing empty positions makes the profile upward closed under taking
supersequences. It does not change the minimum length: delete empty
positions, and merge consecutive positions of the same color when useful.

\begin{theorem}[Ordered normal form]\label{fp:thm:normal}
Whenever a finite layer representation exists,
\[
 \depth(X,c)=\ceil{\log_2\layers(X,c)}.
\]
Moreover, the least possible number of nonempty leaves of a correct
strategy equals \(\layers(X,c)\).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Order the leaves of a decision tree recursively by putting every no-subtree
before the corresponding yes-subtree. We prove that every suffix of this
leaf order has an open union of its point sets. At the root let the open
question be \(U\). A suffix beginning inside the yes-subtree is relatively
open in \(U\), by induction, and is therefore open in \(X\). A suffix
beginning in the no-subtree has the form
\[
 U\cup\bigl((X\setminus U)\cap V\bigr)=U\cup V
\]
for some open \(V\subseteq X\), again by induction using the relative
topology on \(X\setminus U\). Thus the ordered nonempty leaves give a
layer representation. A depth-\(d\) tree has at most \(2^d\) leaves, so
\(\layers(X,c)\leq2^d\).

Conversely, given \(m\) ordered layers, ask membership in a suffix whose
boundary divides the list into two parts of sizes differing by at most one.
Continue recursively on the appropriate interval of positions. Every
question is one of the original globally open suffixes. Balanced binary
search has depth \(\ceil{\log_2m}\) and exactly \(m\) leaves. Taking the
minimum proves both assertions.
\end{proof}

This is the normal-form mechanism used in the repository report
(Part~I, \cref{thm:normal}; Part~II, \cref{fo:thm:normal}); it is reproduced to make the present proof independent of
any formalization or unverified implementation in that repository.

\section{Every finite derived set has a filter model}
\label{fp:sec:filters}

\subsection{The topological decomposition}

\begin{lemma}\label{fp:lem:decomposition}
If \(X\) is Hausdorff and \(X'=\{p_1,\ldots,p_t\}\) is finite, there are
pairwise disjoint clopen neighborhoods \(N_i\) of the \(p_i\) such that
\[
 X=Z\sq N_1\sq\cdots\sq N_t,
\]
where \(Z\) is discrete and clopen and \(N_i'=\{p_i\}\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each pair of distinct \(p_i,p_j\), choose disjoint open neighborhoods.
Intersect the finitely many neighborhoods chosen at each point to obtain
pairwise disjoint open sets \(N_i\), with no other \(p_j\) in \(N_i\).
The complement of \(N_i\) is open: every isolated point in it is an open
singleton, and each \(p_j\), \(j\ne i\), has the disjoint neighborhood
\(N_j\). Hence \(N_i\) is clopen. Their finite union is clopen; its
complement \(Z\) contains no nonisolated point. Finally, an open
neighborhood of a nonisolated point cannot make that point isolated, so
\(N_i'=\{p_i\}\).
\end{proof}

Write \(D_i=N_i\setminus\{p_i\}\). Every point of \(D_i\) is isolated,
and \(D_i\) is infinite. Define
\[
 \F_i=\{A\subseteq D_i:\{p_i\}\cup A\text{ is open in }N_i\}.
\]
This is a proper filter containing all cofinite subsets of \(D_i\). We
use ``free filter'' in precisely this sense. Conversely, for an infinite
set \(D\) and such a filter \(\F\), let \(S(\F)=D\cup\{p\}\), with
all subsets of \(D\) open and the open sets containing \(p\) exactly
\(\{p\}\cup A\), \(A\in\F\). This is a zero-dimensional Hausdorff
space with derivative \(\{p\}\). Indeed, cofinite neighborhoods separate
\(p\) from isolated points, and every basic neighborhood of \(p\) is
clopen.

\subsection{Finite color quotients of a filter}

The dual ideal of \(\F\) is
\[
 \I_{\F}=\{A\subseteq D:D\setminus A\in\F\}.
\]
A set is \(\F\)-positive if it does not belong to \(\I_{\F}\).
Equivalently, it meets every member of \(\F\). For subsets of the
isolated part, this says exactly that \(p\) belongs to their closure.
The ideal contains all finite subsets of \(D\).

For a finite coloring of \(D\), let \(C_b\) be its color cells and put
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:Bdef}
 B=\{b:C_b\text{ is }\F\text{-positive}\}.
\end{equation}
The set \(B\) is nonempty: a finite union of members of a proper ideal
cannot be all of \(D\).

\begin{lemma}[Positive-cell criterion]\label{fp:lem:positive}
For every set \(J\) of colors,
\[
 \bigcup_{b\in J}C_b\in\F\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad B\subseteq J.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If \(B\subseteq J\), the complement is a finite union of nonpositive
cells and belongs to \(\I_{\F}\). Conversely, if \(J\) omits a
positive cell \(C_b\), its union cannot belong to \(\F\), since it is
disjoint from a set meeting every filter member.
\end{proof}

This elementary finite quotient is the key distinction between cofinite
and ultrafilter convergence. For a free ultrafilter, exactly one color
cell is positive. For the cofinite filter on an infinite set, every infinite
color cell is positive.

\section{The complete profile is a directed graph}
\label{fp:sec:profile}

Let \(c:X\to\Sigma\) use exactly the \(q_c\) colors in \(\Sigma_c=c[X]\).
In the clopen decomposition of \cref{fp:lem:decomposition}, set
\[
 a_i=c(p_i),\qquad
 B_i=\{b:c^{-1}(b)\cap D_i\text{ is }\F_i\text{-positive}\}.
\]
Let \(G_c\) be the simple loopless digraph on \(\Sigma_c\) whose arcs
are \(a_i\to b\) for \(b\in B_i\setminus\{a_i\}\). Repeated arcs
are recorded once. An arc \(a\to b\) represents the two-letter
subsequence requirement \(ab\), not a requirement of adjacency in a word.

\begin{theorem}[Exact graph profile]\label{fp:thm:profile}
A finite word \(w\) on \(\Sigma_c\) is admissible for \((X,c)\) if and
only if it contains every color of \(\Sigma_c\) and, for every arc
\(a\to b\) of \(G_c\), it contains \(ab\) as a subsequence.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose first that a layer representation has word \(w\), and \(p_i\)
is in position \(k\). The suffix from \(k\) is an open neighborhood of
\(p_i\). Its intersection with \(D_i\) belongs to \(\F_i\), so it
meets every positive color cell. Thus each \(b\in B_i\) occurs at a
position \(j\geq k\). If \(b\ne a_i\), then \(j>k\), which gives
\(a_ib\) as a subsequence. All used colors must occur somewhere.

Conversely, suppose the word has all these properties. Assign \(p_i\)
to the \emph{first} occurrence of \(a_i\) in \(w\), and assign every
isolated point of color \(b\), including points in \(Z\), to the
\emph{last} occurrence of \(b\). For each \(b\in B_i\), the assigned
position of its isolated points is at least the assigned position of
\(p_i\). This is immediate if \(b=a_i\); otherwise it follows from
the subsequence condition.

Consider any suffix of the resulting assignment. If it does not contain
\(p_i\), its intersection with \(N_i\) is a subset of the discrete part,
so is open. If it contains \(p_i\), it contains all positive color cells
of \(D_i\); \cref{fp:lem:positive} says that its isolated part belongs to
\(\F_i\), so it is again open. The intersection with \(Z\) is open.
Since the decomposition is clopen, the suffix is globally open. The
assignment is color-respecting and proves admissibility.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why arbitrary words do not occur here]
At a point of this first nontrivial derivative level, a finite coloring
creates only two-letter constraints. Higher Cantor--Bendixson levels can
create longer word constraints, which are not encoded by a single digraph
on the colors. The reduction is complete for a finite derived set, not a
purported classification of all scattered spaces.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Two-letter constraints and directed feedback sets}

For a loopless digraph \(G\) on a finite vertex set \(V\), let
\(\ac(G)\) be the largest size of a subset inducing an acyclic digraph.
Then \(\tau(G)=|V|-\ac(G)\). Let \(\ell(G)\) be the shortest length
of a word containing every vertex and every directed edge as a
subsequence.

\begin{lemma}\label{fp:lem:word-fvs}
For every nonempty finite loopless digraph,
\[
 \ell(G)=|V|+\tau(G).
\]
An optimal word can be constructed from any minimum feedback vertex set
and a topological ordering of its complement.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let \(w\) satisfy the requirements, and let \(R\) be the set of vertices
appearing at least twice. Every vertex outside \(R\) appears once. Their
order in \(w\) respects all arcs among them, so \(G[V\setminus R]\)
is acyclic. Consequently \(R\) is a feedback vertex set and
\[
 |w|\geq |V|+|R|\geq |V|+\tau(G).
\]

Conversely, write a minimum feedback set as \(R=(r_1,\ldots,r_k)\),
and let \(T\) be a topological ordering of \(G[V\setminus R]\).
The word
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:word-witness}
 r_1\cdots r_k\;T\;r_k\cdots r_1
\end{equation}
contains every required pair. Arcs inside \(T\) are respected by its
topological order. Arcs from \(R\) to \(T\) use the first block,
and arcs from \(T\) to \(R\) use the last block. Every pair of distinct
vertices of \(R\) occurs in both orders across its two blocks.
The word has length \(|V|+k\). A minimum feedback set has a nonempty
complement, so no degenerate empty middle block is needed.
\end{proof}

The shortest-common-supersequence viewpoint has a substantial independent
literature; see, for example, Lagoutte and Tavenas \cite{LT}. We supply
\cref{fp:lem:word-fvs} as an elementary bridge, not as a claim to have
introduced either supersequences or feedback vertex sets.

\begin{theorem}[Exact coloring complexity]\label{fp:thm:fixed}
For every finite coloring of a Hausdorff space with a finite nonempty
derived set, \cref{fp:eq:main-intro} holds. Both minima are finite, and
\cref{fp:eq:word-witness} followed by balanced suffix search constructs an
optimal strategy from a minimum feedback vertex set.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{fp:thm:profile,fp:lem:word-fvs,fp:thm:normal}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Positive label weights]\label{fp:cor:weights}
Assign each used color a positive weight \(w_b\), and charge this weight
once for each occurrence of the color in a layer word. The minimum weighted
layer cost is
\[
 \sum_{b\in\Sigma_c}w_b+
 \min_{R:\,G_c-R\text{ acyclic}}\sum_{b\in R}w_b.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The repeated vertices of any admissible word form a feedback set, giving
the lower bound. The doubled-feedback-set construction attains it for a
minimum-weight feedback set.
\end{proof}
This is a statement about weighted layer occurrences. It is not a formula
for expected query cost or for arbitrary unequal costs of open questions.

\section{Local partition capacity and exact attainability}
\label{fp:sec:capacity}

\subsection{A local Boolean invariant}

\begin{definition}
The partition capacity \(b(\F)\) of a free filter is the supremum of the
numbers of pairwise disjoint \(\F\)-positive subsets of its underlying
set. Its value lies in \(\N_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}\). Here \(\infty\)
means that such families exist at every finite size; no particular infinite
family is asserted by the notation.
\end{definition}

Disjoint positive sets can be enlarged to a partition by adding the
unassigned remainder to one of them. Thus \(b(\F)\) is also the maximum
possible number of positive color cells, or the statement that this number
is unbounded over finite colorings.

\begin{proposition}\label{fp:prop:finite-capacity}
The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Restricting a filter to one of its members does not change its
partition capacity.
\item \(b(\F)=1\) if and only if \(\F\) is an ultrafilter.
\item If \(b(\F)=b<\infty\), then \(\F\) is the intersection of
exactly \(b\) distinct free ultrafilters, uniquely determined as an unordered
family. Conversely, such an intersection has capacity \(b\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, if \(A\in\F\), its complement belongs to the
dual ideal. Intersecting a positive set with \(A\) preserves positivity.
This transfers disjoint positive families in both directions.

For the remaining assertions, consider the Boolean algebra
\(\B=\PP(D)/\I_{\F}\). Positive sets represent its nonzero elements.
Suppose the largest size of a disjoint nonzero family is the finite number
\(b\). A family of maximum size covers the unit: otherwise its complement
could be appended. Each member is an atom: otherwise splitting it would
give a larger family. Every Boolean-algebra element is a union of some of
these atoms, because intersecting it with an atom gives either that atom
or zero. Thus \(\B\) is the Boolean algebra of subsets of a \(b\)-element
set. Its \(b\) atom evaluations lift to distinct ultrafilters on \(D\).
An element of \(\PP(D)\) lies in all of them exactly when its quotient
class is the unit, which is equivalent to membership in \(\F\).

Conversely, finitely many distinct ultrafilters can be separated by finitely
many sets. The Boolean algebra generated by these sets has a cell belonging
to each ultrafilter and to no other. It follows that the map to the
\(b\) simultaneous truth values is onto \(\{0,1\}^b\), with the
intersection filter as the inverse image of the all-one vector. Its dual
quotient therefore has exactly \(b\) atoms. The case \(b=1\) gives the
second assertion and uniqueness follows from the quotient atoms.
\end{proof}

Consequently \(b_i=b(\F_i)\) is independent of the clopen decomposition:
two chosen neighborhoods have a common neighborhood obtained by
intersection, and restriction does not alter the capacity.

\subsection{Realizable stars and a complete finite optimization}

\begin{lemma}\label{fp:lem:star-realization}
At a point with capacity \(b_i\), any prescribed center color \(a_i\)
and any prescribed set \(A_i\) of at most \(\min(b_i,q-1)\) other
colors can be realized as exactly its outgoing neighbors in the profile
graph. All \(q\) colors may be made to occur globally without changing
any prescribed local graph.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If \(A_i\) is nonempty, partition \(D_i\) into \(|A_i|\) positive
sets and give them the colors in \(A_i\). If \(A_i\) is empty, color
all of \(D_i\) by \(a_i\). Give \(p_i\) its prescribed center color.
Any missing labels can be inserted at finitely many isolated points in one
component, avoiding one reserved representative of every label already used.
Finite sets belong to the dual ideal, and removing finitely many
points from a positive set preserves positivity. Therefore these changes
introduce no positive color cell and destroy none.
\end{proof}

For a list of capacities \(\boldsymbol b=(b_1,\ldots,b_t)\), let
\(\mathcal G_q(\boldsymbol b)\) consist of all loopless digraphs on
\(\{1,\ldots,q\}\) expressible as the union of stars
\[
 \{a_i\to x:x\in A_i\},\qquad
 |A_i|\leq\min(b_i,q-1),\quad a_i\notin A_i.
\]
Centers may repeat and stars may be empty.

\begin{theorem}[Capacity classification]\label{fp:thm:capacity}
For every Hausdorff \(X\) with finite nonempty derived set and local
capacity list \(\boldsymbol b\),
\begin{align}
 \max_{c:X\to[q]}\layers(X,c)
 &=q+\max_{G\in\mathcal G_q(\boldsymbol b)}\tau(G),\label{fp:eq:capacity-L}\\
 \sm_q(X)
 &=\ceil{\log_2\!\left(q+\max_{G\in\mathcal G_q(\boldsymbol b)}\tau(G)\right)}.
 \label{fp:eq:capacity-d}
\end{align}
In particular, \(\sm_q(X)\) depends only on the truncated capacities
\(\min(b_i,q-1)\), not on the underlying infinite cardinalities or the
particular ultrafilters.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every coloring using all \(q\) labels gives a graph in the displayed
class by the definition of capacity. Conversely, every graph in the class
is realized by \cref{fp:lem:star-realization}, using all \(q\) labels.
A coloring using fewer labels can be enlarged to use all labels by finite
changes at isolated points, preserving its graph and adding isolated graph
vertices. By \cref{fp:thm:fixed}, this cannot lower its layer count.
The maxima are over finite graph classes, and the logarithm is monotone,
so \cref{fp:thm:fixed} gives both equalities.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exactly when the rank-one bound is sharp}

Let \(S=\{c(p_i):1\leq i\leq t\}\) and \(s=|S|\). All arcs of
\(G_c\) originate in \(S\), so every directed cycle lies in \(S\).
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:active-centers}
 \tau(G_c)=s-\ac(G_c[S]),\qquad
 \layers(X,c)=q_c+s-\ac(G_c[S])\leq q_c+s-1.
\end{equation}
This recovers the repository bound \((q-1)h+s\) at \(h=1\), when
all \(q\) colors occur.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp local attainment criterion]\label{fp:thm:attainment}
Fix \(q\geq1\) and \(1\leq s\leq\min(q,t)\). There is a coloring
using all \(q\) labels, exactly \(s\) labels on \(X'\), and having
\(\layers(X,c)=q+s-1\), if and only if there is a partition
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:bins}
 \{1,\ldots,t\}=I_1\sq\cdots\sq I_s,
 \qquad I_j\ne\varnothing,\qquad
 \sum_{i\in I_j}b_i\geq s-1\quad(1\leq j\leq s).
\end{equation}
An infinite capacity is interpreted as exceeding every finite threshold.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{fp:eq:active-centers}, equality holds precisely when
\(\ac(G_c[S])=1\). A loopless digraph on \(s\) vertices has acyclic
number one exactly when every pair of distinct vertices is joined by both
arcs: if one arc is missing, that pair is acyclic; conversely every pair in
a complete bidirected graph is a directed two-cycle.

Group the nonisolated points according to their center colors. In a group
\(I_j\), the outgoing stars must cover all \(s-1\) other center colors.
Each star covers at most \(b_i\) of them, so \cref{fp:eq:bins} is necessary.

Conversely, under \cref{fp:eq:bins}, assign center color \(j\) to every
\(p_i\) with \(i\in I_j\). Distribute the \(s-1\) other center
colors among those indices, assigning at most \(b_i\) distinct targets
to index \(i\). This is possible because the total capacity is sufficient;
some indices may receive no targets. Realize these stars by
\cref{fp:lem:star-realization}. The induced graph on the center colors is
complete bidirected. Add any missing labels by finite isolated exceptions.
Then \cref{fp:eq:active-centers} gives exactly \(q+s-1\) layers.
\end{proof}

This is an exact criterion for \emph{layer} sharpness. Because a ceiling
logarithm can identify different layer counts, failure of the criterion
does not automatically imply a smaller worst-case query depth. The next
section isolates a case where the depth really changes.

\section{Three colors: a complete classification and a separation}
\label{fp:sec:ternary}

\begin{theorem}[All finite-derived-set spaces, three colors]\label{fp:thm:ternary}
Let \(X\) be Hausdorff with a finite nonempty derived set. Put
\[
 u=|\{i:b_i=1\}|,\qquad v=|\{i:b_i\geq2\}|.
\]
Then \cref{fp:eq:ternary-intro} holds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every such space is infinite, so some coloring uses all three labels;
at least two queries are then necessary. By \cref{fp:thm:fixed}, at most
five layers, hence three queries, suffice. Three queries are necessary
for a coloring precisely when it needs five layers, which is equivalent
to its graph being complete bidirected on three colors.

Apply \cref{fp:thm:attainment} with \(q=s=3\). We need three nonempty
groups, each of total capacity at least two. Every capacity at least two
can supply one such group by itself; every two capacities equal to one
can supply one group. Thus three groups exist if
\(v+\floor{u/2}\geq3\). Conversely, in any three satisfactory groups,
at most \(v\) groups contain a capacity at least two. Each remaining
group consumes at least two of the \(u\) unit capacities. Hence the
same inequality is necessary. Extra indices can be added to any group.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Two spaces with the same derivative cardinalities}

Choose a free ultrafilter \(\U\) on \(\N\), whose existence is a
standard consequence of ZFC, and let
\[
 X=S(\U)\sq S(\U)\sq S(\U).
\]
Its capacity list is \((1,1,1)\). On the other hand, let
\[
 Y=(\omega+1)\sq(\omega+1)\sq(\omega+1)
\]
with each summand a convergent sequence and its limit. Its local filters
are cofinite and its capacity list is \((\infty,\infty,\infty)\).
Both spaces are countably infinite and zero-dimensional Hausdorff, and
\[
 |X'|=|Y'|=3,\qquad X''=Y''=\varnothing.
\]
Nevertheless \cref{fp:thm:ternary} gives
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:separation}
 \boxed{\sm_3(X)=2\quad\text{and}\quad\sm_3(Y)=3.}
\end{equation}
Thus rank, cardinality, and the cardinality of the last derivative do not
determine this invariant, even in this very small derivative-height class.
The example changes local topology, not the number of nonisolated points.

For an explicit hard coloring of \(Y\), color the three limit points
\(0,1,2\), respectively. In the component with limit color \(a\),
partition the isolated sequence into two infinite sets with the two other
colors. The resulting profile contains all six directed pairs on three
colors. It requires five layers; \(01210\) is an optimal word.

For \(X\), each component contributes at most one arc, so a coloring
has at most three distinct arcs. It cannot contain all six directed pairs.
Therefore four layers always suffice. The coloring with component profiles
\(01,12,20\) gives the cycle \(0\to1\to2\to0\), and the optimal
word \(0120\) has four layers. Writing the nonisolated points as
\(p_0,p_1,p_2\) and their discrete parts as \(D_0,D_1,D_2\), those
layers can be chosen as
\[
 \{p_0\},\quad D_0\sq\{p_1\},\quad
 D_1\sq\{p_2\},\quad D_2.
\]
Balanced suffix search uses two questions. The supplied certificate file
contains both this tree and a five-layer tree for the convergent example.

\subsection{Why the counterexample is not locally compact}

\begin{proposition}\label{fp:prop:ultra-noncompact}
A one-point free-ultrafilter space on \(\N\) is not locally compact at
its nonisolated point and admits no injective sequence converging to it.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let \(A\in\U\). Split \(A\) into two infinite parts. Exactly one
part, say \(B\), belongs to \(\U\). The cover of \(\{p\}\cup A\)
consisting of \(\{p\}\cup B\) and the singleton sets from \(A\setminus B\)
has no finite subcover. Basic neighborhoods are closed, so a compact
neighborhood containing one would make that basic neighborhood compact,
a contradiction.

If an injective sequence converged to \(p\), split its range into the
even- and odd-indexed subsequences. At least one of these two sets is not
in \(\U\); its complement belongs to \(\U\) and gives a neighborhood
of \(p\) omitting infinitely many terms. This contradicts convergence.
\end{proof}

\section{Positive classification under ordinary local hypotheses}
\label{fp:sec:positive}

\begin{proposition}\label{fp:prop:infinite-capacity}
If a nonisolated point of a Hausdorff space with finite derived set is
first countable, its local partition capacity is infinite. The same
conclusion holds if the space is locally compact at that point.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Pass to its one-point clopen neighborhood. In the first-countable case,
choose a decreasing neighborhood base and recursively choose a new isolated
point from each base member. This is possible because a neighborhood of a
nonisolated point in a \(T_1\) space is infinite. The resulting injective
sequence converges to the point. Splitting it into any prescribed finite
number of infinite subsequences gives disjoint positive sets.

In the locally compact case, take a compact neighborhood contained in the
one-point clopen neighborhood. Within that compact subspace, every open
neighborhood of the nonisolated point has finite complement: the complement
is closed, compact, and discrete. Its isolated part is infinite. Partition
that part into arbitrarily many infinite sets. Each accumulates at the
nonisolated point in the compact subspace and hence in the ambient space.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{fp:cor:regular-formula}
Suppose \(X\) is Hausdorff, \(0<|X'|=t<\infty\), and every nonisolated
point is either first countable or has a compact neighborhood. Then for
every \(q\geq1\),
\begin{align*}
 \max_{c:X\to[q]}\layers(X,c)&=q-1+\min(q,t),\\
 \sm_q(X)&=\ceil{\log_2\bigl(q-1+\min(q,t)\bigr)}.
\end{align*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For any coloring, \cref{fp:eq:active-centers} bounds the layers by
\(q-1+\min(q,t)\), also after adjoining unused labels. Every capacity
is infinite by \cref{fp:prop:infinite-capacity}; therefore the partition
criterion is satisfied with \(s=\min(q,t)\). It supplies equality.
Apply \cref{fp:thm:normal}.
\end{proof}

This extends the rank-one convergent-tower formula to all spaces in the
stated classes. Compactness and first countability are sufficient, not
necessary: the exact hypothesis for layer attainment is the capacity
partition criterion.

\begin{example}[Mixed local behavior]
For three labels, capacity lists \((2,2,1)\) and \((2,2,1,1)\) have
worst-case depths two and three, respectively. In the second list the
three required groups are the two single entries of capacity two and the
pair of unit entries. An arbitrarily large capacity at just one point
cannot be divided between several different center colors: each
nonisolated point has only one color. This is why summing all capacities
without the partition requirement would be incorrect.
\end{example}

\section{An exact directed extremal calculation}
\label{fp:sec:extremal}

To obtain a closed formula for unit capacities, we need the minimum number
of arcs in a digraph on \(q\) vertices whose acyclic number is at most
\(r\). Antiparallel arcs are allowed, but loops and multiple arcs are not.
The random-order method below belongs to the Caro--Wei family of arguments;
stronger directed bounds appear in \cite{AGJS,AFH}. Only the elementary
one-sided version is needed here, and we prove it directly.

\begin{lemma}[Random-order bound]\label{fp:lem:random}
For every finite loopless digraph \(G\),
\[
 \ac(G)\geq\sum_{v\in V(G)}\frac{1}{d^+(v)+1}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose a uniformly random ordering of the vertices. Select a vertex if it
appears before all its out-neighbors. Within the selected set, every arc
goes forward in the ordering, so the selected set is acyclic. A vertex of
out-degree \(d\) is selected with probability \(1/(d+1)\). Taking
expectations proves the bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact arc threshold and equality]\label{fp:thm:turan}
Let \(1\leq r\leq q\), and write \(q=ar+s\) with \(0\leq s<r\).
The minimum number of arcs in a loopless digraph on \(q\) vertices with
\(\ac(G)\leq r\) is
\[
 T(q,r)=ra(a-1)+2as.
\]
The graphs attaining this minimum under the stated acyclic-number
constraint are exactly the disjoint unions of \(r\) complete bidirected
graphs whose sizes differ by at most one: \(s\) have size \(a+1\), and
\(r-s\) have size \(a\).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For every nonnegative integer \(d\),
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:integer-support}
 \frac1{d+1}\geq\frac{2a-d}{a(a+1)}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the difference is
\[
 \frac{(d-a)(d-a+1)}{(d+1)a(a+1)}\geq0,
\]
since the numerator is the product of consecutive integers. Equality
holds precisely for \(d=a-1\) or \(d=a\).

If \(m\) is the number of arcs, summing \cref{fp:eq:integer-support} and
using \cref{fp:lem:random} gives
\[
 r\geq\ac(G)\geq\sum_v\frac1{d^+(v)+1}
 \geq\frac{2aq-m}{a(a+1)}.
\]
Thus
\[
 m\geq2aq-ra(a+1)=ra(a-1)+2as=T(q,r).
\]
The union of balanced complete bidirected graphs described in the
statement has acyclic number exactly \(r\) and has exactly that many
arcs, proving sharpness.

It remains to prove the equality classification. If \(m=T(q,r)\) and
\(\ac(G)\leq r\), all inequalities above are equalities. In particular,
the random selected set in \cref{fp:lem:random} has expectation \(r\) and
always has size at most \(r\). It must therefore have size exactly
\(r\) for every ordering.

There can be no asymmetric arc \(u\to v\) with \(v\not\to u\).
Compare an ordering beginning \(u,v\) with the ordering beginning
\(v,u\), leaving all other vertices in the same later order. In the
first ordering both \(u\) and \(v\) are selected. In the second,
only \(v\) is selected. The selection status of every other vertex is
unchanged, since both \(u,v\) remain before it. This changes the total
by one, a contradiction. Thus all arcs occur in opposite pairs.

Regard the resulting symmetric graph as undirected. It cannot contain an
induced path \(u-v-w\) with no edge \(uw\). Put this triple first in
the ordering. The order \(u,w,v\) selects its two endpoints; the order
\(v,u,w\) selects only its middle vertex. All later selection statuses
again remain unchanged. This is impossible. An undirected graph with no
induced three-vertex path is a disjoint union of cliques: otherwise a
shortest path between nonadjacent vertices in a connected component begins
with such an induced path.

Finally, equality in \cref{fp:eq:integer-support} requires every out-degree
to be \(a-1\) or \(a\), so each clique has size \(a\) or \(a+1\).
Its acyclic number is its number of components, which must be \(r\).
The sum of component sizes gives the claimed multiplicities.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is and is not being claimed as new]
The theorem is an elementary directed extremal calculation with familiar
balanced-clique extremizers. Its proof is included in full so the
open-query thresholds do not rely on a possibly mismatched graph citation.
We do not assert historical priority for this standalone graph theorem.
The source search located stronger degree-based acyclic-set bounds, not a
priority certification for this exact reformulation.
\end{remark}

\section{All ultrafilter-sum thresholds}
\label{fp:sec:ultra}

Let
\[
 X_t=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^t S(\U_i),\qquad t\geq1,
\]
where the \(\U_i\) are arbitrary free ultrafilters on infinite sets.
The sets and ultrafilters need not be isomorphic. An additional discrete
summand makes no difference to the following formulas.

\begin{theorem}\label{fp:thm:ultra}
For \(q\geq1\), define \(r_*(q,t)\) as in \cref{fp:eq:ultra-intro}.
Then \cref{fp:eq:ultra-intro} holds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Each component has capacity one and contributes at most one arc. Conversely,
any digraph on \(q\) vertices with at most \(t\) arcs can be realized:
use one component for each arc, give unused components an empty star,
and introduce unused labels by finite exceptions. Therefore
\[
 \max_c\layers(X_t,c)
 =q+\max\{\tau(G):|V(G)|=q,\ |E(G)|\leq t\}.
\]
Since \(\tau(G)=q-\ac(G)\), \cref{fp:thm:turan} says that the minimum
possible acyclic number under this arc budget is exactly \(r_*(q,t)\).
This proves the layer formula. Apply \cref{fp:thm:normal} for depth.
The set defining \(r_*\) is nonempty because \(T(q,q)=0\).
For \(q=1\), the formulas give one layer and zero questions, as required.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact threshold for the extra question}

\begin{corollary}\label{fp:cor:jump}
Let \(q\geq2\) and \(k=\ceil{\log_2q}\). Put
\[
 r_q=2q-2^k-1,\qquad t_q=T(q,r_q).
\]
Then \(1\leq r_q<q\), and
\begin{equation}\label{fp:eq:jump}
 \sm_q(X_t)=
 \begin{cases}
 k,&1\leq t<t_q,\\
 k+1,&t\geq t_q.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Using all \(q\) colors forces at least \(k\) questions. Since
\(\tau(G)\leq q-1\), at most \(2q-1<2^{k+1}\) layers are needed,
so at most \(k+1\) questions suffice. The larger depth occurs exactly
when some graph with at most \(t\) arcs satisfies
\[
 q+\tau(G)>2^k
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \ac(G)\leq2q-2^k-1=r_q.
\]
Apply \cref{fp:thm:turan}. The inequalities for \(r_q\) follow directly
from \(2^{k-1}<q\leq2^k\), including the case that \(q\) is a power
of two.
\end{proof}

The threshold varies substantially with the alphabet size. If \(q\) is a
power of two, \(t_q=2\). If \(q=2^{k-1}+1\), then \(r_q=1\), so
\(t_q=q(q-1)\). Thus the number of components needed for one additional
question ranges from two to a quadratic function of the number of labels.
This variation comes from the exact rounding boundary of a binary tree,
not from a change in derivative height.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
Colors \(q\)&Baseline depth \(k\)&\(r_q\)&First \(t\) requiring \(k+1\)\\
\midrule
2&1&1&2\\
3&2&1&6\\
4&2&3&2\\
5&3&1&20\\
6&3&3&6\\
7&3&5&4\\
8&3&7&2\\
9&4&1&72\\
10&4&3&24\\
11&4&5&14\\
12&4&7&10\\
13&4&9&8\\
14&4&11&6\\
15&4&13&4\\
16&4&15&2\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The archive contains the exact table through \(q=64\)
(shipped as \path{data/03-filter-profiles-query_jump_thresholds.csv}).

\subsection{Binary and ternary checks}

For two colors, \(r_*(2,1)=2\), while \(r_*(2,t)=1\) for \(t\geq2\).
Thus
\[
 \sm_2(X_1)=1,\qquad \sm_2(X_t)=2\quad(t\geq2),
\]
which agrees with the repository's binary distinction between a singleton
and a nonsingleton last derivative.

For three colors,
\[
 \max_c\layers(X_t,c)=
 \begin{cases}
 3,&t=1,\\
 4,&2\leq t\leq5,\\
 5,&t\geq6.
 \end{cases}
\]
The query depth is consequently two for \(1\leq t\leq5\), and three
for \(t\geq6\). In particular, three limit points do not suffice to
force the extra query in the ultrafilter case, whereas they do in the
cofinite case.

\begin{corollary}[Layer saturation with prescribed top colors]
For unit capacities and \(2\leq s\leq\min(q,t)\), the layer value
\(q+s-1\) is attainable with exactly \(s\) colors on the derived set
if and only if \(t\geq s(s-1)\). For \(s=1\), it is always attainable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each of the \(s\) nonempty bins in \cref{fp:eq:bins} needs at least
\(s-1\) unit entries, and this condition is also sufficient.
\end{proof}

\section{Algorithms, input models, and formalization boundaries}
\label{fp:sec:algorithms}

\subsection{An exact finite algorithm}

For a supplied profile graph on \(q\) used labels, enumerate subsets
\(R\) of its vertex set in increasing cardinality. Test whether
\(G-R\) is acyclic by a topological sort. The first successful subset
is a minimum feedback vertex set. Construct the word
\cref{fp:eq:word-witness}, assign nonisolated points to first occurrences and
isolated points to last occurrences as in \cref{fp:thm:profile}, and balance
suffix queries.

A direct implementation uses
\[
 O\bigl(2^q(q+|E|)\bigr)
\]
time for the graph optimization. This bound is independent of the sizes
of the infinite discrete pieces once their finite profiles have been
supplied. Directed feedback vertex set has more sophisticated parameterized
algorithms; \cite{GMM} describes that line of work. Those algorithms are
optional and are not needed for any theorem here.

\paragraph{Essential input qualification.}
We do not claim an algorithm can determine ultrafilter membership for an
arbitrary presented infinite color cell. The finite algorithm takes the
positive-cell sets \(B_i\), or equivalently the graph, as input. In the
explicit extremal constructions the coloring is monochromatic on each
ultrafilter component apart from finitely many exceptions, so these profiles
are known without an oracle. For arbitrary colorings, obtaining the profile
is a separate semantic or effective problem.

\subsection{Why exact optimization can still be difficult}

\begin{proposition}\label{fp:prop:complexity}
For finite graph-profile input with an unbounded alphabet, deciding whether
\(\layers\leq M\) is NP-complete. Deciding whether \(\depth\leq d\) is
also NP-complete. These statements already hold for profiles realizable by
finite sums of one fixed free-ultrafilter space, using colorings constant
on each discrete component outside finitely many exceptions.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A feedback vertex set and a topological ordering of its complement are
polynomially checkable certificates. By \cref{fp:thm:fixed}, the layer
question with \(M=q+k\) is exactly the directed feedback vertex set
question \(\tau(G)\leq k\). The latter is a classical NP-complete
problem; see the discussion and references in \cite{GMM}. Every graph
profile is realizable by one ultrafilter component per arc.

For depth, it is necessary to account for rounding rather than merely
quote the layer reduction. Given \(G\) on \(q\) vertices and a
nontrivial bound \(0\leq k\leq q-1\), let \(P\) be the least power
of two satisfying \(P\geq q+k\), and adjoin
\[
 z=P-q-k
\]
isolated graph vertices. If \(G'\) is the resulting graph, then
\(\tau(G')=\tau(G)\), and
\[
 \depth(G')\leq\log_2P
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 q+z+\tau(G)\leq P
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \tau(G)\leq k.
\]
The number \(z\) is \(O(q)\), so the construction is polynomial.
Unused colors are realized at finite isolated exceptions. A dummy
empty-star component may be included when needed. Membership in NP follows
from the same certificate, with the integer inequality
\(q+|R|\leq2^d\); when \(d\) is unnecessarily large the instance is
immediately positive by the universal \(2q-1\) layer bound.
\end{proof}

Unlike the general reduced-word supersequence setting studied in
\cite{LT}, our profiles have only two-letter constraints. This is complexity
for an explicit finite profile encoding, not for an unspecified encoding of
arbitrary topological spaces or infinite sets.
For every fixed \(q\), the graph search has a fixed finite bound.

\begin{writenotec}[Relation to Part~II's hardness theorem]
The last sentence does not conflict with Part~II's \cref{fo:thm:NP}, by
which exact optimization is NP-complete for every fixed $q\ge3$. The input
models differ: there the instances are finite posets (disjoint chains
already suffice) or succinct tower lists, whose words have unbounded length, and the difficulty is the
shortest common supersequence of those words; here the input is a profile
graph on the colors, which for a space with finite nonempty derived set
has at most $q(q-1)$ arcs, so for fixed $q$ only finitely many inputs exist.
\Cref{fp:prop:complexity} is a second NP-completeness route, through
directed feedback vertex set with an unbounded alphabet.
\end{writenotec}

\subsection{A concrete formalization route}

The argument separates naturally into four interfaces. First formalize
ordered words, subsequences, open suffixes, and the tree-to-layer normal
form. Second formalize digraphs, topological orders, and the repeated-letter
proof of \(\ell(G)=q+\tau(G)\). Third formalize the finite partition
lemma for filters and its graph-profile bridge. Finally add the
finite-derived-set decomposition, the Boolean-capacity argument, and the
random-order extremal theorem.

The graph portions admit purely finite certificates. A positive certificate
consists of a feedback set, a topological order, an admissible word, and a
balanced tree. A negative certificate can list a directed cycle in every
induced subgraph that would contradict the proposed feedback lower bound,
or use an independently verified exhaustive subset search. The filter
existence and topology must remain separate mathematical obligations.
The present archive contains no \texttt{.lean} or \texttt{.v} file and
makes no kernel-verification claim.

\begin{writenotec}[Status in ProveIt]
None of these interfaces has been formalized in ProveIt: no Lean or Rocq
file of the repository mentions open-query games, filter capacities or
directed feedback vertex sets, and the Lean library in the enclosing
\path{SetTheory/Cardinals} directory is about large cardinals.
\end{writenotec}

\section{Reproducible checks and proof audit}
\label{fp:sec:verification}

\subsection{Independent finite computations}

Run the standard-library Python program, with the shipped name and an
explicit output directory, as
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 code/03-filter-profiles-verify.py --output <scratch>
\end{lstlisting}
It enumerates all loopless directed graphs on up to four labeled vertices,
allowing antiparallel arcs. Independently, it enumerates all reduced words
through length \(2q-1\), records their covered ordered pairs, and performs
a supermask-minimum transform. That computes the shortest admissible word
for every target graph without calling the feedback-set routine. The two
answers are then compared.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
\toprule
Vertices \(q\)&Digraphs checked&Reduced words enumerated\\
\midrule
1&1&1\\
2&4&6\\
3&64&93\\
4&4,096&4,372\\
\midrule
Total&4,165&4,472\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

For every graph, the verifier also constructs a doubled-feedback-set word
and checks all required subsequences directly. It checks the extremal arc
threshold for every \(1\leq r\leq q\leq4\), and verifies that every
equality graph has the balanced complete-bidirected-component structure.

There are also \textbf{11,515 positive-cell word checks}: direct existence
of a common permitted position for the nonisolated point is compared with
the pairwise subsequence requirements. A further \textbf{98 capacity
cases} compare exhaustive star-union realizations with an independent
bin-assignment test for \cref{fp:thm:attainment}. Finally, exact threshold
tables and balanced extremal constructions are generated through 64 colors.
The recorded run passed every assertion.

These numbers describe finite evidence, not the scope of the theorems.
In particular, no finite experiment can instantiate a free ultrafilter on
\(\N\), prove the clopen decomposition for arbitrary Hausdorff spaces,
or establish historical novelty.

option. Here \path{code/verify.py} is Part~I's verifier, so the command

\end{itemize}
The counts above agree with the recorded JSON, which also records ten extremal

\end{writenotec}

The substantive novelty proposed for further independent review is the
identification of the finite-output topological invariant with local filter
capacities and the resulting exact separation and threshold laws. The
literature check is not exhaustive. The mathematical statements do not
depend on a claim of priority.

\section{Further research questions and topics}
\label{fp:sec:questions}

The first two directions continue the original repository question beyond
the class settled here. The remaining directions arise from the exact
classification above; they are proposed problems, not assertions that each
formulation is a previously published open problem.

\begin{question}[The next derivative level]\label{fp:q:next-level}
For Hausdorff spaces with finite nonempty second derivative and empty third
derivative, what finite local data replace the positive-color stars?
Can an iterated filter quotient encode all admissible words without
exponentially listing every lower-level profile?
\end{question}
The obstruction is correlation: a neighborhood may contain whole lower-level
profiles, rather than independent color cells. Merely attaching a scalar
capacity to each point is unlikely to record all such interactions; a proof
or a counterexample would sharply delimit this extension.

\begin{writenoted}[Bears on this question]
For a fixed coloring of a Hausdorff space with $X'''=\varnothing$, the
closure-signature words of length at most three are complete data for
admissibility (\cref{cw:lem:derivative,cw:thm:duality}), and
\cref{cw:prop:hierarchy} at $r=3$ gives two three-colorings of one compact
space with $X'''=\varnothing$ that agree on every word of length at most two
but need $7$ and $5$ layers, so the words of length three cannot be dropped.
Part~IV gives no capacity or local-star form of these data, and does not
treat the worst case over colorings; the question stays open.
\end{writenoted}

\begin{question}[Compact and first-countable finite-height spaces]\label{fp:q:compact-height}
Does the repository bound \((q-1)h+s\) attain its full layer value under
natural compactness or first-countability hypotheses at every finite height?
The present paper proves this at \(h=1\), not at larger heights.
\end{question}
A promising approach is to construct enough incompatible colored profiles
inside disjoint neighborhoods while controlling their accumulation at the
next derivative. The proof must not assume metrizability or a countable
local base when neither is available.

\begin{question}[Intermediate-capacity extremal formulas]\label{fp:q:intermediate}
Find a closed formula for
\[
 \max_{G\in\mathcal G_q(b_1,\ldots,b_t)}\tau(G)
\]
when some capacities lie strictly between one and \(q-1\). Determine
whether suitable unions of bidirected cliques always optimize any important
uniform-capacity subcase.
\end{question}
For a common finite capacity \(b\geq1\), a graph is realizable exactly when
\(\sum_x\ceil{d^+(x)/b}\leq t\), with zero out-degree contributing
zero. This follows by splitting each outgoing neighborhood into at most
\(b\)-element stars. It converts the question into a discrete degree-cost
extremal problem, but the linear-arc proof of \cref{fp:thm:turan} does not
by itself solve the nonlinear cost problem.

\begin{question}[Infinite derived sets]\label{fp:q:infinite}
Which Hausdorff spaces with an infinite discrete derived set admit a useful
filter-component decomposition for finite-output games? When such a global
decomposition fails, can local profiles still determine the finite-depth
invariant?
\end{question}
The finite proof uses pairwise disjoint neighborhoods chosen for finitely
many nonisolated points. Replacing ``finite'' by ``infinite'' without a
separation argument would be invalid.

\begin{writenoted}[Second half answered for fixed colorings when $X''=\varnothing$]
Part~IV, which does not cite this question, answers its second half for every
fixed coloring of a Hausdorff space whose derived set is discrete, that is,
$X''=\varnothing$: then $\layers(X,c)=q_c+\tau(G_c)$, where $G_c$ has
an arc $a\to b$ exactly when some point of color $a$ lies in the closure of
the color class of $b$ (\cref{cw:thm:rankone}). No filter-component
decomposition, compactness or first countability is used. Which spaces admit
a useful decomposition, and the worst case $\sm_q$ over colorings of such
spaces, are not addressed.
\end{writenoted}

\begin{question}[Expected query cost]\label{fp:q:expected}
For a probability distribution on the hidden point, characterize the minimum
expected depth over all admissible layer assignments and ordered binary
trees. How does the optimum depend on masses of positive and null color
cells in the filter quotient?
\end{question}
The weighted-layer identity in \cref{fp:cor:weights} is only a preliminary
algebraic analogue. Expected depth depends on leaf masses, and redistributing
a color among repeated positions can change those masses.

\begin{question}[Effective presentation of local profiles]\label{fp:q:effective}
For computably presented filters and color cells, when can the positive-color
sets \(B_i\) be computed or approximated? Which oracle strength is necessary
for exact strategy synthesis in natural presentations?
\end{question}
This isolates the semantic task deliberately excluded from the finite
feedback-set algorithm. Even a tiny profile graph need not be effectively
recoverable from arbitrary infinite-set data.

\begin{question}[Near-extremal stability]\label{fp:q:stability}
If \(\ac(G)\leq r\) and \(|E(G)|\) is close to \(T(q,r)\), must
\(G\) be close to a balanced union of complete bidirected graphs, in a
quantitative sense useful for almost-sharp query families?
\end{question}
The equality proof supplies two concrete defects: asymmetric arcs and
induced three-vertex paths. A stability theorem would need to control how
many such defects can coexist with small slack in the random-order bound.

\begin{question}[Recovering topology from the alphabet spectrum]\label{fp:q:spectrum}
How much of a finite capacity list can be recovered from the sequence
\((\sm_q(X))_{q\geq1}\)? Does recording maximum layer counts instead
of rounded query depths make the capacity multiset identifiable?
\end{question}
The ceilings erase information: at three labels, one ultrafilter component
has maximum layer count three, whereas two through five components have
maximum layer count four, yet all have depth two. This suggests studying
the unrounded layer spectrum first.

\begin{question}[Choice strength and definability]\label{fp:q:choice}
What is the weakest set-theoretic assumption supporting a countable
three-color separation of the kind in \cref{fp:eq:separation}? Can comparable
separations be obtained from explicitly definable neighborhood filters under
additional descriptive restrictions?
\end{question}
The example here uses a free ultrafilter and is an existence construction
in ZFC, not an explicitly computable list of open neighborhoods. Nothing
in the finite certificate files resolves this foundational issue.

\section{Conclusion}
\label{fp:sec:conclusion}

At the first nontrivial derivative level, the relevant local information is
not merely the existence of an accumulation point. It is the family of
color cells that may accumulate there simultaneously. A finite coloring
turns that information into directed pair constraints; the cost of making
those constraints compatible is exactly a feedback vertex number.

This gives a complete fixed-color profile theorem for every Hausdorff space
with a finite nonempty derived set, an exact local criterion for attainment
of the rank-one bound, a full three-label classification, and all
alphabet-dependent thresholds for ultrafilter sums. The resulting
three-color separation shows concretely why the binary
Cantor--Bendixson classification does not extend unchanged to finite output
alphabets. Higher derivative levels, infinite derived sets, and general
cost models remain distinct problems.
