\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status (manuscript 05).} Part~IV is an AI-assisted, unrefereed
research manuscript. Its new claims are ordinary mathematical theorems proved
in it, not Lean- or Rocq-checked results; it ships executable finite checks
but no proof-assistant files, and nothing in it is formalized in ProveIt. It
credits as prior material the tree--layer normal form, greedy elimination,
the chain interpretation, the compact word construction and the
universal-word constant of Parts~II and~III, and claims as its contribution
their extension through closure-word duality and its consequences. ``New
here'' means new relative to the inspected report, not certified historical
priority; its literature checks were targeted, not exhaustive. It does not
claim to settle the uniform-coloring or transfinite questions of the cited
literature: it does not determine which signatures the colorings of a
prescribed space can have, so the attainability half of
\cref{fo:q:invariants} stays open. It does not identify word equivalence with
Wadge equivalence or with any partition hierarchy, and does not claim the
shortest-common-supersequence problem or its three-letter hardness
\cite{LT}. Its algorithms are relative to stated closure and emptiness
operations, not polynomial-time algorithms for arbitrary topological input;
its product law concerns the specified pair coloring, not the supremum over
colorings of a product; its obstruction bound is an upper bound, not claimed
optimal. The finite checks test finite instances only; every infinite-space
statement rests on the written proofs, and the recorded run time of
4.282~seconds is specific to the delivering environment.
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{The manuscript's title block and abstract}
\label{cw:sec:abstract}
\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Closure-Word Duality for Open-Query Games. Universal finite models,
optimal topological compression, finite-budget certificates, and an exact
product law.} ProveIt research series. AI-assisted research manuscript
prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov, 30 September 2026.

A hidden point of a topological space is to be assigned one of finitely many
known labels, using adaptive questions about open sets. We associate to the
labeling a language of reduced words detected by iterated color closures.
We prove, without separation assumptions, that a proposed layer word is
admissible exactly when it contains every detected word as a subsequence.
Moreover, failure of an $m$-letter proposal has a detected counterword of
length at most $m$. This converts every finite-budget problem, on an arbitrary
space, into a finite shortest-common-supersequence problem.

For finite-depth games the detected language is a finite downset, uniquely
recoverable from the entire admissibility profile. Every such downset is
realized both by a finite union of colored chains and by a compact countable
metrizable space. Its longest word has length equal to the least possible
Cantor--Bendixson height of a Hausdorff realization. We obtain an exact
feedback-vertex-set formula when the second derivative is empty, including
spaces with infinitely many nonisolated points; an improved finite-poset
obstruction bound; and the product law
$\rho(X\times Y,c\times e)=\rho(X,c)+\rho(Y,e)-1$.
A pair of three-colorings of the same compact space agree on all patterns
of length at most three and on every derivative's color cardinalities, yet
need respectively three and four queries. Full proofs, an explicit algorithm,
a research agenda, and independent exhaustive finite checks accompany the
article.

\textbf{Status and scope.}
The new claims are ordinary mathematical theorems proved in this manuscript,
not Lean- or Rocq-checked results. The repository's tree--layer normal form,
greedy elimination test, chain interpretation, compact word construction,
and universal-word constant are credited as prior material. The contribution
is their extension through closure-word duality and its consequences.
Historical priority has not been independently certified. This is not a claim
to settle all uniform-coloring or transfinite questions in the cited
literature.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Notation of Part~IV}
\label{cw:sec:notation}
Part~IV fixes a nonempty space $X$, with no separation axiom unless stated, a
finite alphabet $\Sigma$ of $q$ letters and a coloring $c:X\to\Sigma$, as
Part~II does; $A_a=c^{-1}(a)$. Several letters are used differently in
Part~IV and in Parts~I--III. Where a false reading is tempting, the table
prints it beside the true one.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.14\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.45\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Part~IV & Parts~I--III\\
\midrule
\endhead
$\cwSig(X,c)$, $\rho(X,c)$ & The closure signature, the nonempty reduced
words $v$ with $F_v\ne\varnothing$, and its rank, the supremum of their
lengths (\cref{cw:sec:signature}). On a finite poset $\cwSig(P,c)=\C(P,c)$
(\cref{cw:lem:poset}). & Part~II: $\C(P,c)$ the reduced chain words of a
poset; $h_c$ in \cref{fo:cor:height} is $\rho(P,c)$.\\
$T_a$, $R_a$, $F_v$ & $T_a(F)=\cwcl{A_a\cap F}$, the color closure;
$R_a(F)=\cwcl{F\setminus A_a}$; $F_v$ the nested closure along $v$.
\emph{False:} $T_a$ is Part~II's automaton transition; $F_v$ is a layer or a
filter. & Part~II: $T_a(I)$, a transition on order ideals
\eqref{fo:eq:ideal-transition}; Part~I: $F_i$ closed layers; Part~III: $\F$ a
filter, $T(q,r)$ an arc count.\\
$\cwcl{B}$ & The closure of $B$ (the manuscript's one-argument \verb|\cl|). &
Part~I: $\cl$; Part~II: $\cl_X$.\\
$A_a$ & The color class $c^{-1}(a)$. & Part~I: $A$ the target; Part~II:
$\A_{q,m}(S)$ a word family, $A$ a mask.\\
$\W$, $\layers$, $\depth$ & Admissible words, least layer count, least
worst-case depth: the manuscript's $\mathcal W$, $L$ and
$\operatorname{depth}$. & Part~II's $\W$, $\layers$, $\depth$ (same
notions); Part~I's $L(A,X)$.\\
$\cwBas(X,c)$, $B$ & The maximal antichain of a finite signature (the
manuscript's $\mathcal B$); $B$ also a finite antichain or basis in
\cref{cw:thm:finitemodel,cw:thm:classification} and \cref{cw:sec:algorithms}.
\emph{False:} Part~III's $\B$. & Part~III: $\B=\PP(D)/\I_\F$ a Boolean
algebra; $B_i$, $B$ sets of colors.\\
$B(q,m)$, $B_*(q,m)$ & $B(q,m)$ the least obstruction size, as in Part~II's
Research question~3; $B_*(q,m)=mq(q-1)^{m-1}$ the bound
\eqref{cw:eq:budgetsize}. & Part~II: the bound $(m+1)q^m$
(\cref{fo:thm:obstruction}).\\
$\Tw_k$, $\Tw_b$ & The tower of height $k$ (the manuscript's $K_k$), and the
tower colored by a rank word $b$, reduced or not. Its new point has
neighborhoods containing all but finitely many copies, so it is Part~II's
$\Tw_k$. \emph{Not} Part~I's $K_k$. & Part~I: $K_k=[0,\omega^{2^k-1}]$;
Part~II: $\Tw_m$, $c_w$.\\
height, $h$ & The Cantor--Bendixson height, the least $k$ with
$X^{(k)}=\varnothing$; it is $h+1$ for the last derivative rank $h$. The
space $Z$ of height four has $h=3$. \emph{False:} ``height'' $=h$. & $h$ the
last derivative rank; Appendix~B: the height is $h+1$.\\
$D$, $D_m$ & A finite downset of reduced words; $D_m$ the signature
truncated at length $m$; in the proof of \cref{cw:thm:rankone}, a topological
ordering. \emph{Not} the depth $\depth$. & Part~I: $D_j$; Part~II: $\depth$;
Part~III: $D_i$, $D$ isolated parts.\\
$U(q,r)$, $U_i$, $U_j$ & $U(q,r)=(q-1)r+1$, Part~II's
$\scs(\A_{q,r})$ \eqref{fo:eq:universal-all}; blocks $U_i$ in
\cref{cw:lem:separator}; open suffixes $U_j$ \eqref{cw:eq:questions}. &
Part~II: $U_j$ open suffixes.\\
$N(q,r)$ & The number of nonempty reduced words of length at most $r$. &
Part~I: $N_0,N_1$; Part~II: $N$ an input length.\\
$G_c$, $q_c$, $\tau$ & As in Part~III: arcs $a\to b$ for
$A_a\cap\cwcl{A_b}\ne\varnothing$; for a finite derived set this is
Part~III's digraph. $G_t$ is the elimination chain, as in Part~II. &
Part~III: $G_c$, $q_c$, $\tau$; Part~II: $G_t$.\\
$r$, $s$, $e$, $\Gamma$ & $r$ a rank; in \cref{cw:thm:product}, $r,s$ the
factor ranks and $e:Y\to\Gamma$ the second coloring. \emph{False:} $s$ is a
number of colors. & Part~II: $s$ colors on $X^{(h)}$; Part~III: $r$ a bound
on $\ac$, $s$ a remainder.\\
$t$, $\ell_i$ & The number and the lengths of basis words in
\cref{cw:sec:algorithms}. & Part~III: $t=\lvert X'\rvert$, $\ell(G)$.\\
$Z$, $c_{\rm hard}$, $c_{\rm soft}$ & The sum of 24 towers of
\cref{cw:thm:separationexample} and its two colorings. & Part~III: $Z$ the
clopen discrete part.\\
$\eps$, $\preceq$ & The empty word; the subsequence relation. & Part~II
writes $\subseq$ for the same relation.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{Parts~I--III inside Part~IV}
\label{cw:sec:crosswalk}
The following checks were made when Part~IV was added; they are consequences
of the four parts' proofs, not new theorems.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Normal form and elimination.} \Cref{cw:thm:normal} and
\cref{cw:prop:elimination} are \cref{fo:thm:normal} and
\cref{fo:prop:closure}; the chain \eqref{cw:eq:elimination} is
\eqref{fo:eq:closure}, and at $q=2$ both are Part~I's \cref{thm:normal}.
\item \emph{Posets.} On a finite poset, \cref{cw:lem:poset} identifies the
signature with Part~II's reduced chain words $\C(P,c)$, so
\cref{cw:thm:duality} there is \cref{fo:thm:chains}, and
\eqref{cw:eq:rankbounds} is \eqref{fo:eq:height-bound} with $h_c=\rho(P,c)$.
Part~IV extends both to every topological space, and adds the truncation to
constraints of length at most $\len{w}$.
\item \emph{Finite height.} On a Hausdorff space of last derivative rank $h$,
\cref{cw:lem:derivative} gives $\rho\le h+1$, so \eqref{cw:eq:rankbounds}
gives $\layers\le(q-1)(h+1)+1=(q-1)h+q$. This is weaker than Part~II's
sharp \cref{fo:thm:CB}, $(q-1)h+s$ with $s\le q$, as the manuscript says after
\cref{cw:cor:rankbounds}.
\item \emph{Rank one.} For a Hausdorff space with a finite nonempty derived
set, the arcs \eqref{cw:eq:arc} are those of Part~III's $G_c$ (a point $p_i$
of color $a$ in the closure of $A_b$), so \eqref{cw:eq:graphformula} is
Part~III's \cref{fp:thm:fixed}. \Cref{cw:thm:rankone} assumes only
$X''=\varnothing$: the derived set may be infinite, and no compactness,
first countability or filter decomposition is used. It concerns a fixed
coloring; Part~III's capacity results on $\sm_q$ are not extended.
\item \emph{Obstructions.} For finite posets \cref{cw:cor:obstruction}
improves the bound $(m+1)q^m$ of \cref{fo:thm:obstruction} to
$mq(q-1)^{m-1}$; for example $12$ instead of $27$ points at $q=3$, $m=2$.
\Cref{fo:thm:obstruction} is stated for every poset,
\cref{cw:cor:obstruction} for finite ones.
\item \emph{Products.} The rank law \eqref{cw:eq:productrank} is the
coloring analogue of Part~I's \cref{lem:product}; see the note after
\cref{cw:thm:product}. The binary phenomenon of Appendix~\ref{cw:app:certificates}
(chains $01$ and $10$) is the finite-poset form of the clash of orientations behind Part~I's counterexample (\cref{sec:small}).
\item \emph{Separations.} \Cref{cw:thm:separationexample} and Part~III's
\eqref{fp:eq:separation} separate different things: Part~III compares two
spaces through $\sm_3$; Part~IV compares two colorings of one space whose
signatures agree up to length three. Neither contradicts the other, and no
statement of Parts~I--III is refuted or retracted.
\item \emph{Independent checks.} A script written at intake and rerun at this
write (not shipped) enumerated all 389 labelled topologies on at most four
points and their three-colorings and reproduced the counts of
\cref{cw:sec:verification} (29,577 colorings, 21,639 with a finite tree, 109
distinct finite signatures). It compared duality and the short-certificate
test with a brute-force search for layerings, which checks suffix openness
directly and does not use elimination, on every coloring with at most three
points and a random 2\% of those with four (73,452 comparisons in the rerun,
69,612 at intake; all agree). It confirmed $U(q,r)=(q-1)r+1$ by exact
shortest-supersequence search for $(q,r)=(2,3),(2,5),(3,2),(3,3),(3,4),(4,2),(4,3)$;
the layer counts $9$ and $7$ and the basis sizes $24$ and $12$ of
\cref{cw:thm:separationexample}; the product signature and the rank law on
300 random pairs of two-colored preorders with at most three points; and
$\layers=q+\tau(G)$ on all 4,165 loopless digraphs on at most four labelled
vertices. The worked certificates of Appendix~\ref{cw:app:certificates} were
checked by hand.
\end{enumerate}

\section{The problem, the repository frontier, and the contribution}
\label{cw:sec:introduction}

Suppose a point $x$ of a known space $X$ is hidden. One may ask whether $x$
belongs to any chosen open subset of $X$, and later questions may depend on
earlier answers. The objective is not necessarily to identify $x$: it is to
output its known color $c(x)$ in a finite alphabet $\Sigma$.
The central issue is how topology constrains the order in which colors can
be separated. Ordinary cardinalities of color classes do not capture that
constraint.

Chiozini, Csern\'ak and Soukup study a broader, transfinite cut-and-choose
framework and associated point-separating and set-membership invariants
\cite{CCS}. Their arXiv record was checked at version 3, dated 29 May 2026.
We work only with finite worst-case budgets and with a specified coloring.
These restrictions are substantive: a supremum over all colorings and a
transfinite game need additional arguments.

\subsection{The inspected baseline}

The direct baseline is the ProveIt report \emph{Open-query games on ordinal
spaces}, Parts I--III (printed above), at commit
\begin{center}\small\texttt{a4268e78ebd0f6bf71ba609c07f4b3415748f532}.\end{center}
Its article is the Git blob
\texttt{d1e50fd006a8ba1fe645b41b4fb37ee35f7043fe}.
Part II identifies open-query trees with ordered monochromatic layers,
represents finite colored posets by chain-word constraints, and realizes
individual words on compact convergent towers. Part III treats spaces with
finite nonempty derived set by local filters and a directed graph on colors.

\Cref{fo:q:invariants} in Part~II asks for exact invariants beyond convergent
towers and for a characterization through accumulating word profiles.
Its Part III annotation explicitly leaves higher finite heights and infinite
derived sets beyond the finite-derived-set treatment. The present work
answers the \emph{fixed-coloring profile} part in full generality. It does not
determine which profiles can be produced by all colorings of each prescribed
space. That remaining realizability problem includes the uniform local-capacity
question raised in the same research program.

\begin{writenoted}[Scope: ``in full generality'']
The phrase refers to the second half of \cref{fo:q:invariants}, for one
coloring at a time: on every space, the admissible words of a fixed
coloring are characterized by its closure signature
(\cref{cw:thm:duality,cw:thm:recovery,cw:thm:classification}). The first
half, attainability of $(q-1)h+s$ over all colorings of a given space,
stays open beyond Part~III's class, as the next sentence says; the
question is therefore answered in part, and the note after
\cref{fo:q:invariants} records the new scope.
\end{writenoted}

The comparison is targeted. We inspected the report's definitions, normal-form
and elimination arguments, synchronization and complexity discussion,
research questions, and finite-derived-set results. We did not audit every
mathematical assertion in ProveIt. The supplied source audit, shipped here as
\path{04-closure-words-SOURCES.md}, records
paths, the pin, and the inspected ranges.

\subsection{A map of the results}

For a closed set $F\subseteq X$ and color $a$, put
\[
 T_a(F)=\cwcl{F\cap c^{-1}(a)}.
\]
For a word $v=v_1\cdots v_k$, let
$F_v=T_{v_1}\cdots T_{v_k}(X)$, with operators acting from right to left.
The \emph{closure signature} consists of the nonempty reduced words $v$ for
which $F_v\ne\varnothing$. Reduced means that adjacent letters are different;
letters may recur later.

The main results, with the precise hypotheses given below, are these.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Universal duality and short certificates.}
A word $w$ is admissible exactly when every word of the closure signature is
a subsequence of $w$. If $w$ has $m\ge1$ letters, only signature words of
length at most $m$ need be tested (\cref{cw:thm:duality}).
\item \textbf{Complete finite-depth classification.}
Finite-depth games, identified when they admit exactly the same words, are
classified by nonempty finite downsets of reduced words. Their unique maximal
antichains give exact finite SCS instances. They have finite-poset and compact
countable metrizable realizations; the maximum signature length is the
optimal Hausdorff realization height (\cref{cw:thm:classification,cw:thm:minheight}).
\item \textbf{Consequences beyond the previous local graph model.}
The graph formula extends to every Hausdorff space with $X''=\varnothing$.
Finite budgets have uniformly bounded finite replicas and improved actual
subposet obstructions. Independent products have additive signature rank,
minus one. Higher-order closures cannot be replaced by pairwise or triple
summaries (\cref{cw:thm:rankone,cw:thm:budgetmodel,cw:thm:product,cw:thm:separationexample}).
\end{enumerate}

The finite-topology checks compare independent minimax optimization with the
word and closure calculations. They include all labeled topologies on at most
four points and all their three-colorings. They are tests of finite instances,
not a substitute for any infinite-space proof.

\subsection{Novelty boundary and related frameworks}

The repository already supplies the normal form and the single-word closure
elimination test used in \cref{cw:sec:normal}. Those are not claimed as new here.
Neither is the SCS problem or its hardness for reduced words over three
letters: the latter is established by Lagoutte and Tavenas \cite{LT}.
Selivanov's work on difference and fine hierarchies of finite partitions
provides a broader descriptive-set-theoretic context \cite{Selivanov}.
We do not identify our equivalence relation with Wadge equivalence or claim
that existing partition hierarchies were previously unavailable.

The demonstrated extension is an explicit, all-space duality for the entire
admissibility language, its finite-budget truncation, a recovery theorem,
optimal-height realization, and the stated consequences. These are the
mathematical claims to assess. A literature-wide priority determination would
require a more extensive comparison than the source review performed here.

\section{Open-query trees and ordered layers}
\label{cw:sec:normal}

Throughout, $X$ is nonempty and $\Sigma$ is a finite nonempty alphabet.
The coloring $c:X\to\Sigma$ need not be continuous or surjective. Unless
otherwise specified, there is no separation assumption.
Write $A_a=c^{-1}(a)$, and let $q=|\Sigma|$. Unused colors may be discarded
when improving a numerical bound.

\begin{definition}[Admissibility]
For $m\ge1$, a word $w=w_1\cdots w_m$ is \emph{admissible} for $(X,c)$ if
there exists $\lambda:X\to\{1,\ldots,m\}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:layering}
 c(x)=w_{\lambda(x)},\qquad
 \{x:\lambda(x)\ge j\}\text{ is open for every }j.
\end{equation}
Empty fibers are allowed. Let $\W(X,c)$ be the set of admissible words and
\[
 \layers(X,c)=\min\{\len{w}:w\in\W(X,c)\},
\]
with value $\infty$ when the set is empty. Let $\depth(X,c)$ be the minimum
worst-case depth of a correct finite binary open-query tree, or $\infty$.
\end{definition}

Inserting empty layers preserves admissibility. Adjacent layers of the same
color can be merged, because the suffix sets at the surviving boundaries
remain open. Thus a shortest admissible word has no equal adjacent letters.
We use $v\preceq w$ for the ordinary scattered-subsequence relation.

\begin{theorem}[Tree--layer normal form; repository baseline]
\label{cw:thm:normal}
The minimum number of nonempty leaves of a correct finite open-query tree is
$\layers(X,c)$. If finite, then
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:depth}
 \depth(X,c)=\ceil{\log_2 \layers(X,c)}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Order a tree's leaves recursively, with the no-subtree before the yes-subtree.
Every suffix of its leaf cells is open. To see this, use induction in the
current possible-point subspace. If the current query is $U$, a suffix wholly
in the yes-subtree is $U\cap V$ for a relatively open $V$. A suffix meeting
the no-subtree contains all of the yes-subtree and is
\[
 U\cup((X\setminus U)\cap V)=U\cup V.
\]
The subspace version of the same identity proves the induction. Remove empty
leaves. Correctness makes each remaining cell monochromatic, so its position
is a layering. Conversely, a layering with $m$ nonempty fibers permits balanced
binary search of its index using the open suffixes in \eqref{cw:eq:layering}.
This gives $m$ leaves and depth at most $\ceil{\log_2m}$.
A depth-$d$ binary tree has at most $2^d$ leaves, which proves the converse
inequality and the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Greedy elimination; repository baseline]
\label{cw:prop:elimination}
For closed $F$ put $R_a(F)=\cwcl{F\setminus A_a}$. Given
$w=w_1\cdots w_m$, define
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:elimination}
 G_0=X,\qquad G_{t+1}=R_{w_{m-t}}(G_t)\quad(0\le t<m).
\end{equation}
Then $w\in\W(X,c)$ if and only if $G_m=\varnothing$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $\lambda$ is a layering for $w$. Its prefixes
$H_t=\{\lambda\le m-t\}$ are closed. Induction gives $G_t\subseteq H_t$:
a point of $H_t$ with color different from $w_{m-t}$ must belong to
$H_{t+1}$, and closure preserves containment in this closed set.
Thus $G_m\subseteq H_m=\varnothing$.

Conversely, the $G_t$ decrease and are closed. If $G_m=\varnothing$, give
$G_t\setminus G_{t+1}$ the index $m-t$. Such a point has color $w_{m-t}$,
since $G_t\setminus A_{w_{m-t}}\subseteq G_{t+1}$. The index-$\ge j$ suffix
is $X\setminus G_{m-j+1}$, which is open. These layers partition $X$ and
prove admissibility.
\end{proof}

Both statements are reproduced to make the new argument independent of an
unformalized external lemma. Their prior appearance is Part II,
\cref{fo:thm:normal,fo:prop:closure} of the pinned report.

\section{The closure signature}
\label{cw:sec:signature}

\begin{definition}[Color closures and detected words]
For a closed $F\subseteq X$, define
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:colorclosure}
 T_a(F)=\cwcl{A_a\cap F},\qquad
 F_{a_1\cdots a_k}=T_{a_1}\bigl(T_{a_2}(\cdots T_{a_k}(X)\cdots)\bigr),
 \qquad F_{\eps}=X.
\end{equation}
Let $\red(v)$ be obtained from $v$ by replacing each nonempty constant run
by one letter. The \emph{closure signature} is
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:signature}
 \cwSig(X,c)=\{v\ne\eps:v=\red(v),\ F_v\ne\varnothing\}.
\end{equation}
Its rank is
\[
 \rho(X,c)=\sup\{\len{v}:v\in\cwSig(X,c)\}\in\N\cup\{\infty\}.
\]
Since $X\ne\varnothing$, its signature is nonempty and its rank is at least one.
\end{definition}

The expression $F_{abc}$ records a genuinely nested condition:
\[
 F_{abc}=\cwcl{A_a\cap\cwcl{A_b\cap\cwcl{A_c}}}.
\]
It is not the intersection of the three individual color closures. A point
need not witness all stages by a chain of actual specialization comparisons;
in Hausdorff spaces nontrivial stages arise from accumulation instead.

\begin{lemma}[Operator calculus]\label{cw:lem:operators}
On the lattice of closed subsets, each $T_a$ is monotone, contractive,
idempotent, and preserves finite unions. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:reductiondownset}
 F_v=F_{\red(v)},\qquad v\preceq u\Longrightarrow F_u\subseteq F_v.
\end{equation}
The signature is a downset among nonempty reduced words ordered by subsequence.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Monotonicity follows from monotonicity of intersection and closure.
Since $F$ is closed, $T_a(F)\subseteq F$. Also
$A_a\cap F\subseteq A_a\cap T_a(F)$, whereas $T_a(F)\subseteq F$ gives the
reverse containment after taking the corresponding closures. Hence
$T_a(T_a(F))=T_a(F)$. Finite-union preservation follows from
$\cwcl{B\cup C}=\cwcl B\cup\cwcl C$.

Idempotence removes repeated adjacent operators. Deleting an operator from
a composition can only enlarge its value: the deleted operator is
contractive and all operators outside it are monotone. Iterating deletions
proves the second assertion of \eqref{cw:eq:reductiondownset}, and hence the
downset property.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why reduction is essential]
If $a$ occurs as a color, then $F_{a^k}=F_a\ne\varnothing$ for every $k$.
Counting unreduced word lengths would therefore make every rank infinite.
The reduction removes this artificial repetition, while retaining meaningful
patterns such as $aba$.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Reduction and subsequences]\label{cw:lem:stutter}
For a reduced word $v$ and an arbitrary word $w$,
\[
 v\preceq w\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad v\preceq\red(w).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
One implication follows because $\red(w)\preceq w$. For the other, the
positions used by an embedding of a reduced word cannot use two letters
from a single constant run: between two equal letters of $v$ a different
letter must occur. Map each used run to its single representative.
\end{proof}

\section{Closure-word duality and short failure certificates}
\label{cw:sec:duality}

The topological step is finite distributivity; the combinatorial step is a
precise relation between subsequence failure and coordinatewise avoidance.
We prove the latter first.

\begin{lemma}[Diagonal avoidance]\label{cw:lem:diagonal}
Let $w,b\in\Sigma^m$, with $m\ge1$, and assume $b_i\ne w_i$ for every $i$.
Then $\red(b)\not\preceq w$.
Conversely, if a nonempty reduced word $v$ is not a subsequence of $w$, there
exists such a $b$ with $\red(b)\preceq v$ and
$\len{\red(b)}\le m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For the first direction put $v=\red(b)$, and let $j_i$ be the index of the
constant run of $b$ containing position $i$. Let $p_i$ be the length of the
prefix of $v$ matched greedily while scanning $w_1\cdots w_i$.
We claim $p_i<j_i$. At the first position, $w_1\ne b_1=v_1$, so $p_1=0$.
If a new run starts at $i$, then $j_i=j_{i-1}+1$ and $p_i\le p_{i-1}+1<j_i$.
If the run does not change, reaching $j_i$ at step $i$ would require
$w_i=v_{j_i}=b_i$, which is excluded. This proves the claim. At $i=m$,
$p_m<\len v$, so greedy matching fails, equivalently $v\not\preceq w$.

For the converse, greedily match the given $v$ in $w$, and let $p_i$ be the
matched prefix length \emph{after} scanning position $i$. Since matching
never finishes, set $b_i=v_{p_i+1}$. If there was no advance at position $i$,
the definition of greedy matching gives $b_i\ne w_i$. If there was an
advance, then $w_i=v_{p_i}$ and $b_i=v_{p_i+1}\ne v_{p_i}$ because $v$ is
reduced. The indices $p_i+1$ are nondecreasing and increase only by one.
Thus $\red(b)$ is a contiguous segment of $v$, in particular a subsequence,
and has at most $m$ letters.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Universal closure-word duality]\label{cw:thm:duality}
For every topological space and finite coloring as above, and every word
$w$ of length $m\ge1$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{align}
 &w\in\W(X,c);\label{cw:eq:dual1}\\
 &v\preceq w\quad\text{for every }v\in\cwSig(X,c);\label{cw:eq:dual2}\\
 &v\preceq w\quad\text{for every }v\in\cwSig(X,c)
       \text{ with }\len v\le m.\label{cw:eq:dual3}
\end{align}
In particular, each rejected $m$-letter word has a rejected constraint
$v\in\cwSig(X,c)$ of length at most $m$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since the color classes form a finite partition,
\[
 R_a(F)=\cwcl{\bigcup_{b\ne a}(F\cap A_b)}
       =\bigcup_{b\ne a}T_b(F).
\]
All $T_b$ preserve finite unions. Therefore the final residual in
\eqref{cw:eq:elimination} expands as
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:expansion}
 G_m=R_{w_1}\cdots R_{w_m}(X)
     =\bigcup_{\substack{b\in\Sigma^m\\ b_i\ne w_i\ (1\le i\le m)}}F_b.
\end{equation}
An empty index set means an empty union, including the one-color case.

If $w$ is rejected, \cref{cw:prop:elimination} says $G_m\ne\varnothing$.
Some term $F_b$ in \eqref{cw:eq:expansion} is nonempty. By
\cref{cw:lem:operators}, $\red(b)\in\cwSig(X,c)$; by
\cref{cw:lem:diagonal}, it is not a subsequence of $w$, and its length is at
most $m$. Thus \eqref{cw:eq:dual3} implies \eqref{cw:eq:dual1}.

Conversely, suppose some $v\in\cwSig(X,c)$ is not a subsequence of $w$.
The converse of \cref{cw:lem:diagonal} gives an indexed word $b$ in
\eqref{cw:eq:expansion} with $\red(b)\preceq v$. Then
\[
 \varnothing\ne F_v\subseteq F_{\red(b)}=F_b\subseteq G_m.
\]
So $w$ is rejected. This proves \eqref{cw:eq:dual1}$\Rightarrow$\eqref{cw:eq:dual2}.
The remaining implication \eqref{cw:eq:dual2}$\Rightarrow$\eqref{cw:eq:dual3} is
immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A nontrivial truncation]
The assertion that length-$m$ candidates need only length-$\le m$ constraints
is stronger than the observation that a finite-depth game has finitely many
constraints. It applies even when the signature is unbounded and no finite
strategy exists. A constraint longer than $m$ cannot be embedded into $w$;
the theorem shows that its effect is already witnessed by some shorter
\emph{occurring} constraint. This is a topological consequence of the
operator calculus and the avoidance lemma, not an arbitrary property of
all infinite languages.
\end{remark}

\section{Finite depth, finite antichains, and sharp bounds}
\label{cw:sec:rank}

\begin{theorem}[Finite rank criterion]\label{cw:thm:finiterank}
The following are equivalent: $\depth(X,c)<\infty$, $\layers(X,c)<\infty$,
and $\rho(X,c)<\infty$. If their rank is $r$, the signature is finite, and
its unique set $\cwBas(X,c)$ of subsequence-maximal words satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:scs}
 \layers(X,c)=\scs\bigl(\cwBas(X,c)\bigr),\qquad
 \depth(X,c)=\ceil{\log_2\scs\bigl(\cwBas(X,c)\bigr)}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If an admissible word has length $m$, duality implies that every signature
word has length at most $m$, hence the rank is finite. Conversely, a bounded
language over a finite alphabet is finite. Concatenating all its words gives
a common supersequence, hence an admissible word by duality. A finite downset
is generated by its unique maximal antichain; its common supersequences are
exactly those of that antichain. Now apply \cref{cw:thm:normal}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform size bounds]\label{cw:prop:basisbound}
For $q\ge2$ and finite rank $r$, put
\[
 N(q,r)=q\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}(q-1)^j.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:basisbound}
 |\cwSig|\le N(q,r),\qquad
 |\cwBas|\le q(q-1)^{r-1},\qquad
 \sum_{v\in\cwBas}\len v\le rq(q-1)^{r-1}.
\end{equation}
In particular, at fixed $q,r$ there are at most $2^{N(q,r)}$ different
finite-depth admissibility profiles of rank at most $r$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
There are $q(q-1)^{k-1}$ reduced words of length $k$. This proves the first
bound. Extend each member of the antichain to a reduced word of length $r$
by appending letters different from the preceding letter. Choose any fixed
deterministic extension rule. Two equal extensions would make one of their
original words a prefix of the other, contradicting the antichain property.
Thus extension is injective, proving the second bound and then the third.
The final claim simply counts subsets of the finite pool of possible words;
not all those subsets are signatures of rank at most $r$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Universal reduced words; repository constant]
\label{cw:lem:universal}
For $q\ge2,r\ge1$, the shortest common supersequence of \emph{all} reduced
$q$-letter-alphabet words of length $r$ has length
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:universal}
 U(q,r)=(q-1)r+1.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use the prefix of length $U(q,r)$ of the periodic word
$012\cdots(q-1)012\cdots$. A greedy first match uses at most $q$ positions;
each subsequent, different letter costs at most $q-1$ positions. The last
match is therefore at most $q+(r-1)(q-1)=U(q,r)$.

For the lower bound, in any proposed universal word select a color whose
first occurrence is latest. If a color is absent the word already fails;
otherwise that occurrence has position at least $q$. Subsequently select,
among the $q-1$ different next colors, one whose next occurrence is latest.
If one is absent beyond the current position the word fails again; otherwise
the position increases by at least $q-1$. This recursively chooses a reduced
$r$-letter word whose greedy last match occurs no earlier than $U(q,r)$.
Greedy matching is earliest possible, so the lower bound follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Rank controls queries]\label{cw:cor:rankbounds}
For $q\ge2$ and finite rank $r$,
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:rankbounds}
 r\le \layers(X,c)\le(q-1)r+1.
\end{equation}
The upper bound is attained by a finite union of chains, and by a compact
countable metrizable colored space. For one active color, $r=\layers=1$ and
$\depth=0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A longest constraint gives the lower bound. Every reduced word of length at
most $r$ extends to one of length $r$ when $q\ge2$, so \cref{cw:lem:universal}
and duality prove the upper bound. The realization results of
\cref{cw:sec:realization} realize the downset of all reduced words of length
at most $r$, for which \cref{cw:lem:universal} is sharp.
\end{proof}

The constant in \eqref{cw:eq:universal} is already proved in Part II of the
repository report. Here it becomes a bound for the closure rank of arbitrary
spaces. It should not be confused with the sharper repository bound involving
the number of colors on the last nonempty derivative of a finite-height
Hausdorff space.

\section{Recovering the signature from admissibility}
\label{cw:sec:recovery}

Duality says that the signature determines all admissible words. A converse
is needed if the signature rank is to measure an intrinsic property of the
game, rather than an artifact of its particular topological presentation.
The following elementary separator provides that converse at finite depth.

\begin{lemma}[A supersequence avoiding one forbidden word]
\label{cw:lem:separator}
Let $\cwBas$ be a finite family of reduced words, and let $v=a_1\cdots a_k$
be a nonempty reduced word. If $v\not\preceq b$ for every $b\in\cwBas$,
there exists a reduced word $w$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:separator}
 b\preceq w\ (b\in\cwBas),\qquad v\not\preceq w,\qquad
 \len w\le\sum_{b\in\cwBas}\len b+k-1.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Greedily match $v$ in each $b$. Since matching fails, it yields a decomposition
\[
 b=u_1a_1u_2a_2\cdots u_j a_j u_{j+1},\qquad j<k,
\]
where $u_i$ contains no occurrence of $a_i$. Fill the remaining stages with
empty words. For each stage $i$, concatenate the $u_i$ blocks of all family
members in a fixed order, obtaining $U_i$. It still contains no $a_i$.
Set
\[
 w^*=U_1a_1U_2a_2\cdots U_{k-1}a_{k-1}U_k.
\]
Every $b$ embeds into $w^*$ using its stage blocks and the displayed letters.
Greedy matching of $v$ into $w^*$ matches exactly the displayed first $k-1$
letters, because $U_i$ contains no $a_i$, and then stops. Hence $v$ is avoided.
There are at most $\sum_b\len b$ letters in the blocks and $k-1$
displayed letters. Reduce $w^*$; by \cref{cw:lem:stutter} this preserves every
reduced input constraint, still avoids $v$, and cannot increase length.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Observational completeness at finite depth]
\label{cw:thm:recovery}
If $\depth(X,c)<\infty$, then
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:recover}
 \cwSig(X,c)=\{v\ne\eps:v=\red(v),\ v\preceq w
               \text{ for every }w\in\W(X,c)\}.
\end{equation}
Thus two finite-depth colored spaces over the same alphabet have identical
admissibility languages if and only if they have identical closure signatures.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
One inclusion is duality. If a reduced $v$ is absent from the signature, it
is not a subsequence of any maximal signature word: the signature is a
downset. Apply \cref{cw:lem:separator} to its finite maximal basis. The resulting
$w$ contains every basis word, so is admissible by duality, but avoids $v$.
This proves the other inclusion. The equivalence follows immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Word equivalence]
Colored spaces over a fixed alphabet are \emph{word-equivalent} if their
admissibility languages agree. We use this term only for this explicitly
defined relation, not for homeomorphism, continuous reducibility, or equality
of a single optimal-depth number.
\end{definition}

A scalar optimum loses information even when finite: different antichains
can have the same SCS length. The entire admissibility language retains the
signature. By contrast, when no finite strategy exists, the admissibility
language is empty, and \eqref{cw:eq:recover} must not be used. For example,
an indiscrete two-color component together with an isolated third-color point
and a three-color indiscrete space both have empty admissibility languages,
but their closure signatures differ.

\section{Finite and compact realizations: a complete classification}
\label{cw:sec:realization}

\subsection{Finite posets and actual chains}

Give a finite poset its upper-set topology. The closure of a subset is its
downward closure. We orient a chain from its least to its greatest point.

\begin{lemma}[Occurrences in finite posets]\label{cw:lem:poset}
For a finite colored poset $P$ and word $v=v_1\cdots v_k$,
\[
 F_v=\{x:\exists x\le x_1\le\cdots\le x_k,
                     \ c(x_i)=v_i\text{ for all }i\}.
\]
For reduced $v$, its occurrence is equivalent to a strict chain whose color
word is $v$. In particular, the signature of a chain colored by a reduced
word $b$ is exactly the set of nonempty reduced subsequences of $b$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Induct on the word length, using that $T_a(F)$ is the downward closure of
the $a$-colored points of $F$. Adjacent different colors exclude equality
between adjacent witnesses, so a reduced word gives a strict chain.
The final assertion specializes the witness description to a linearly
ordered set.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Sums]\label{cw:prop:sums}
For any nonempty family of nonempty colored spaces over the same alphabet,
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:sums}
 \cwSig\left(\bigsqcup_i X_i,\bigsqcup_i c_i\right)=\bigcup_i\cwSig(X_i,c_i),
 \qquad
 \W\left(\bigsqcup_i X_i,\bigsqcup_i c_i\right)=\bigcap_i\W(X_i,c_i).
\end{equation}
Their signature rank is the supremum of the component ranks, in
$\N\cup\{\infty\}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Closure in a topological sum is componentwise, so every $F_v$ restricts to
the corresponding $F_v$ on each component. Nonemptiness is therefore
existential over components. This proves the signature assertion; the word
assertion follows from duality, or by gluing layerings for a common word.
\end{proof}

The intersection statement is already in Part II of the repository. The
signature description makes its compatibility information into a finite
antichain calculation whenever the component ranks have a common finite bound.

\begin{theorem}[Finite models]\label{cw:thm:finitemodel}
Let $D$ be a nonempty finite downset of nonempty reduced words, and let
$B$ be its maximal antichain. The disjoint union of the chains colored by
the words $b\in B$ has signature exactly $D$. Every finite-depth colored
space is therefore word-equivalent to a finite union of chains. At alphabet
size $q\ge2$ and rank $r$, it needs at most
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:finitevertices}
 rq(q-1)^{r-1}
\end{equation}
vertices by this construction.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The chain signatures are the principal downsets generated by the words of
$B$, by \cref{cw:lem:poset}. Their union is $D$, by \cref{cw:prop:sums}.
Apply this to the finite signature of a finite-depth game and use duality.
The vertex count is $\sum_{b\in B}\len b$, bounded in
\cref{cw:prop:basisbound}.
\end{proof}

The model need not be a subspace or a continuous image of the original
space. Its defining guarantee is preservation of all admissible words.
This distinction is indispensable for Hausdorff source spaces, as discussed
in \cref{cw:sec:budget}.

\subsection{Compact convergent towers}

Here is a self-contained version of the compact word realization already
used in Part II of the repository. We include nonreduced rank words, which
will be useful for the comparison example.

Let $\Tw_1$ be a singleton. Recursively, form $\Tw_{k+1}$ from countably many
pairwise disjoint clopen copies of $\Tw_k$ and a new point $p$. Neighborhoods
of $p$ contain all but finitely many entire copies; inside each copy use its
own topology. For a word $b=b_1\cdots b_k$, color the root of $\Tw_k$ by $b_1$
and each lower copy recursively by the tail $b_2\cdots b_k$. Call this
colored space $\Tw_b$.

\begin{lemma}[Topology and word profile of a tower]\label{cw:lem:tower}
The space $\Tw_k$ is compact, countable, metrizable and Hausdorff. It has
Cantor--Bendixson height $k$, with a singleton $(k-1)$st derivative.
For every nonempty rank word $b$, reduced or not,
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:towerprofile}
 w\in\W(\Tw_b)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \red(b)\preceq w.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Countability and Hausdorff separation follow inductively. For compactness,
an open cover has a member containing the root and all but finitely many
copies; compactness of those finitely many remaining copies gives a finite
subcover. An explicit metric can be constructed inductively with diameter
at most one. Number the copies by $n\ge1$, set the distance from their
points to the root to $2^{-n}$, the distance between different copies $n,m$
to $2^{-n}+2^{-m}$, and scale the old metric within copy $n$ by $2^{-n}$.
This is a metric and induces exactly the stated topology.

Taking derivatives inside clopen copies commutes with restriction. Until
their last nonempty derivatives disappear, infinitely many copies still
meet every neighborhood of the new root. Induction therefore gives the
root alone at derivative $k-1$ and the empty set at derivative $k$.

For \eqref{cw:eq:towerprofile}, induction on $k$ suffices. The singleton case
asks exactly that its color occur in $w$. Let the root color be $a=b_1$.
If a layering assigns the root position $i$, then $w_i=a$ and its open
suffix $\{\lambda\ge i\}$ contains an entire lower copy. The restriction to
that copy is a layering over the suffix $w_i\cdots w_m$. By induction,
$\red(b_2\cdots b_k)$ embeds there. If its first letter differs from $a$,
its first match is strictly after $i$, so prepend the root's match at $i$.
If its first letter equals $a$, no extra letter is needed for $\red(b)$.
This proves necessity.

For sufficiency, take an embedding of $\red(b)$ into $w$. Give consecutive
levels with the same color the same embedded position. The root has the
smallest used position; within each lower copy use the inductively supplied
layering. A suffix containing the root is the whole space, and every other
suffix is a union of open suffixes within clopen copies. This proves
admissibility.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Classification and compact representatives]
\label{cw:thm:classification}
For a fixed finite alphabet, word-equivalence classes of nonempty finite-depth
colored spaces are in bijection with nonempty finite downsets of nonempty
reduced words. The signature gives the bijection. Every class has both a
finite-poset representative and a compact countable metrizable representative.
For a class of rank $r$, the latter can be chosen as a finite sum of towers
of heights at most $r$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A finite-depth game has a finite downset signature by \cref{cw:thm:finiterank}.
The signature distinguishes its word-equivalence class by
\cref{cw:thm:recovery}. Every finite downset is realized by \cref{cw:thm:finitemodel}.
For its maximal antichain $B$, take the finite disjoint union of the spaces
$\Tw_b$, $b\in B$. By \cref{cw:lem:tower}, its admissibility language consists
exactly of common supersequences of $B$. It is therefore word-equivalent
to the finite model. It has finite depth, so \cref{cw:thm:recovery} also gives
its signature exactly, not merely its optimum. Compactness, countability
and metrizability survive a finite disjoint union.
\end{proof}

The single-word construction is inherited. What turns it into a universal
classification is the new fact that \emph{every} finite-depth topological
game has a bounded, complete word signature.

\section{Derivative bounds and optimal topological compression}
\label{cw:sec:height}

For a Hausdorff space $X$, write $X^{(0)}=X$ and
$X^{(j+1)}=(X^{(j)})'$, where the prime denotes the set of nonisolated
points in the subspace. The finite Cantor--Bendixson height is the least
positive $k$ with $X^{(k)}=\varnothing$, when such a $k$ exists.
Thus a nonempty discrete space has height one.

\begin{lemma}[One derivative per color change]\label{cw:lem:derivative}
For a reduced word $v$ of length $k$ on a Hausdorff colored space,
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:derivative}
 F_v\subseteq X^{(k-1)}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, a space of finite height $h+1$ has $\rho(X,c)\le h+1$
for every finite coloring.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We use the elementary fact that an isolated point of $\cwcl B$ belongs to $B$:
an open set meeting $\cwcl B$ only in that point must meet $B$.
For $k=1$, \eqref{cw:eq:derivative} is immediate.
Write $v=au$ where $u$ starts with $b\ne a$. The set
$F_u=\cwcl{A_b\cap F_{\text{tail}(u)}}$ is closed, and every isolated point
of $F_u$ has color $b$. Therefore
\[
 A_a\cap F_u\subseteq(F_u)'.
\]
Derived sets of closed subspaces of a Hausdorff space are closed in the
ambient space. Taking closures and using induction and monotonicity of
formation of derived sets gives
\[
 F_v=T_a(F_u)\subseteq(F_u)'
       \subseteq\bigl(X^{(k-2)}\bigr)'=X^{(k-1)}.
\]
If the height is $h+1$, words of length $h+2$ cannot occur, nor can longer
ones, by the downset property.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal Hausdorff realization height]\label{cw:thm:minheight}
Let $(X,c)$ be any nonempty finite-depth colored space of rank $r$; the
original space need not be Hausdorff. Among its word-equivalent Hausdorff
representatives of finite Cantor--Bendixson height, the least height is
exactly $r$. The minimum is attained by a compact countable metrizable
space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A word-equivalent representative has finite depth and the same signature,
hence the same rank $r$, by \cref{cw:thm:recovery}. If its height is $k$, then
\cref{cw:lem:derivative} gives $r\le k$. The tower union of
\cref{cw:thm:classification} has height equal to the largest basis-word length,
which is $r$. This attains the lower bound.
\end{proof}

This height is a property of the full finite-query behavior, not of the
underlying uncolored space. A highly complicated or nonscattered space with
a simple continuous coloring may have rank one and a discrete representative.
Conversely, the same fixed space may carry word-inequivalent colorings of
different signature ranks.

\subsection{The exact formula when the second derivative is empty}

Part III of the repository gives a graph formula under a finite-derived-set
hypothesis. Duality removes that finiteness condition for each specified
coloring.

\begin{theorem}[Rank-one topology, with arbitrary derived-set cardinality]
\label{cw:thm:rankone}
Suppose $X$ is Hausdorff and $X''=\varnothing$. Let $q_c$ be the number of
colors actually used. Form the loopless directed graph $G_c$ on those colors
with arc $a\to b$ for $a\ne b$ exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:arc}
 A_a\cap\cwcl{A_b}\ne\varnothing.
\end{equation}
Let $\tau(G_c)$ be its minimum directed feedback vertex number: the least
number of vertices whose deletion leaves an acyclic digraph. Then
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:graphformula}
 \layers(X,c)=q_c+\tau(G_c),\qquad
 \depth(X,c)=\ceil{\log_2(q_c+\tau(G_c))}.
\end{equation}
No compactness, first-countability, or finiteness of $X'$ is assumed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{cw:lem:derivative}, only words of length one or two can occur.
Singleton words are exactly the active colors. A reduced pair $ab$ occurs
exactly when \eqref{cw:eq:arc} holds, since
$F_{ab}=\cwcl{A_a\cap\cwcl{A_b}}$ is nonempty exactly when its preclosure set is.
By duality, $\layers$ is the SCS length of these singleton and arc constraints.

Let $w$ be any common supersequence and let $R$ be the colors occurring at
least twice. The colors outside $R$ occur once; their positions give a linear
order in which every arc between them must point forward. Thus $G_c-R$ is
acyclic, $|R|\ge\tau(G_c)$, and
$\len w\ge q_c+|R|\ge q_c+\tau(G_c)$.

For the reverse bound take a minimum feedback set $R$, list its colors in
any order, and let $D$ be a topological ordering of the remaining vertices.
The word $RDR$ contains every arc: arcs within $D$ use that ordering, arcs
starting in $R$ use its first copy, arcs ending in $R$ its last copy, and
arcs within $R$ use the first and last copies. Every active color occurs.
The length is $q_c+|R|$, as required. Apply \cref{cw:thm:normal} for depth.
\end{proof}

The SCS--feedback-vertex argument is the same finite mechanism as in Part III;
its extension to arbitrary $X'$ is justified by the all-space signature
and derivative theorems. For height at least three, the directed graph is
generally insufficient: length-three and higher closure constraints carry
additional information.

\section{Finite-budget replicas and improved actual obstructions}
\label{cw:sec:budget}

The finite-rank hypothesis can be dropped when only bounded budgets are to
be preserved. This also sharpens the repository's finite-poset obstruction
bound, which was $(m+1)q^m$ in Part II, Research question 3 and its preceding
obstruction discussion.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform finite-budget models]\label{cw:thm:budgetmodel}
Let $(X,c)$ be an arbitrary nonempty colored space, with $q\ge2$, and let
$m\ge1$. There is a finite colored poset $P_m$, with at most
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:budgetsize}
 B_*(q,m)=mq(q-1)^{m-1}
\end{equation}
points, such that
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:budgetequivalence}
 w\in\W(P_m)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad w\in\W(X,c)
 \qquad(1\le\len w\le m).
\end{equation}
There is also a compact countable metrizable representative of this bounded
word behavior with height at most $m$. These models preserve every query
budget $d$ satisfying $2^d\le m$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Truncate the signature to the finite downset
$D_m=\{v\in\cwSig(X,c):\len v\le m\}$, and take its maximal antichain.
Realize $D_m$ by \cref{cw:thm:finitemodel,cw:thm:classification}. The antichain
bound in \cref{cw:prop:basisbound}, with parameter $m$, proves
\eqref{cw:eq:budgetsize}; the compact model uses towers of height at most $m$.

For a candidate of length $k\le m$, duality with its short-certificate
clause says that admissibility on $X$ is determined by $\cwSig_{\le k}$.
Those words belong to $D_m$. Equivalently, $w$ contains all of $D_m$ exactly
when it is admissible on $X$: the forward implication uses the short test,
and the reverse follows from full duality. This proves
\eqref{cw:eq:budgetequivalence}. Finally, a depth-$d$ game exists exactly when
some admissible word has length at most $2^d$, by \cref{cw:thm:normal}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Actual finite subposet obstructions]\label{cw:cor:obstruction}
If a finite $q$-colored poset $P$ has $\layers(P,c)>m\ge1$, with $q\ge2$,
there is an induced subposet $Q\subseteq P$ of at most
$mq(q-1)^{m-1}$ vertices with $\layers(Q,c|_Q)>m$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For every reduced candidate word $w$ of length $m$, choose a signature word
$v_w$ of length at most $m$ not embedded in $w$, using \cref{cw:thm:duality}.
By \cref{cw:lem:poset}, select a chain in $P$ colored by $v_w$. Let $Q$ be
the induced subposet on the union of all selected chains. There are
$q(q-1)^{m-1}$ candidates, so the asserted size bound holds. Each selected
chain remains a chain in $Q$, and therefore still excludes its candidate.

If $Q$ admitted a word of length at most $m$, first reduce it and then
append different successive letters until its length is $m$. Insertion of
empty layers would preserve admissibility, producing one of the excluded
reduced candidates. This contradiction proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why a Hausdorff subspace version would be false]
A finite subspace of a Hausdorff space is discrete. Its restricted coloring
has an admissible word listing each active color once, hence at most $q$
layers. A Hausdorff game needing more than $q$ layers cannot have that
behavior witnessed by a finite subspace with the induced topology.
The finite-budget replica is an abstract colored poset, not such a subspace.
Its infinite compact counterpart preserves accumulation instead.
\end{remark}

The new bound is an upper bound, not a claim of optimality. Its improvement
over the previous $(m+1)q^m$ is due to two changes: a failed length-$m$
candidate now has a constraint of length at most $m$, and it is enough to
enumerate reduced candidates rather than all words.

\section{Products and an additive rank law}
\label{cw:sec:products}

For colorings $c:X\to\Sigma$ and $e:Y\to\Gamma$, the product coloring is
\[
 (c\times e)(x,y)=(c(x),e(y))\in\Sigma\times\Gamma.
\]
This section concerns this specified pair coloring. It does not concern the
supremum over arbitrary colorings of the product space.
For a pair word $v$, write $\pi_Xv$ and $\pi_Yv$ for its coordinate words;
they need not be reduced even when $v$ is.

\begin{theorem}[Exact product signature and rank]\label{cw:thm:product}
For every pair of nonempty colored topological spaces and every nonempty
reduced word $v$ over $\Sigma\times\Gamma$,
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:productsignature}
 v\in\cwSig(X\times Y,c\times e)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \red(\pi_Xv)\in\cwSig(X,c)\ \text{and}\
 \red(\pi_Yv)\in\cwSig(Y,e).
\end{equation}
If the factor ranks are finite, then
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:productrank}
 \boxed{\ \rho(X\times Y,c\times e)=\rho(X,c)+\rho(Y,e)-1.\ }
\end{equation}
If at least one factor rank is infinite, the product rank is infinite.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The closure of a rectangle in a product topology is the rectangle of its
closures. For closed $F\subseteq X$, $H\subseteq Y$, and colors $(a,b)$,
\[
 T^{X\times Y}_{(a,b)}(F\times H)
 =\cwcl{(A_a\cap F)\times(e^{-1}(b)\cap H)}
 =T^X_a(F)\times T^Y_b(H).
\]
Induction on word length gives the stronger set identity
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:rectangle}
 F^{X\times Y}_v=F^X_{\pi_Xv}\times F^Y_{\pi_Yv}.
\end{equation}
Nonemptiness and \cref{cw:lem:operators} prove \eqref{cw:eq:productsignature}.

Let the finite factor ranks be $r,s$. A reduced pair word of length $k$
has $k-1$ transitions, each changing at least one coordinate. The two
coordinate words have respectively at most $r-1$ and $s-1$ changes when
the pair word occurs. Therefore
$k-1\le(r-1)+(s-1)$, proving the upper bound $k\le r+s-1$.

Choose occurring reduced factor words $a_1\cdots a_r$ and
$b_1\cdots b_s$. Follow a lattice path from $(1,1)$ to $(r,s)$, advancing
one coordinate at a time, and record the color pairs $(a_i,b_j)$ along
it. There are $r+s-1$ recorded positions. Adjacent pairs differ because
the changed factor word is reduced; the reduced coordinate projections
are exactly the two chosen words. By \eqref{cw:eq:productsignature} this
pair word occurs, proving the lower bound.

If one rank is unbounded, take arbitrarily long occurring words in that
factor and pair them with any one active color of the other. The same
identity gives unbounded product rank.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Quantitative product consequences]\label{cw:cor:productbounds}
For finite-depth factors,
\begin{align}
 \max\{\layers(X,c),\layers(Y,e)\}
 &\le \layers(X\times Y,c\times e)\le \layers(X,c)\layers(Y,e),\label{cw:eq:productlayers}\\
 \max\{\depth(X,c),\depth(Y,e)\}
 &\le\depth(X\times Y,c\times e)
 \le\depth(X,c)+\depth(Y,e).\label{cw:eq:productdepth}
\end{align}
The least finite Hausdorff realization height of the product game's word
profile is $r+s-1$. More generally, for $n$ finite-rank factors it is
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:nproduct}
 1+\sum_{i=1}^n(r_i-1).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Fixing one coordinate restricts a product query tree to a factor, because
slices of open sets are open. Pruning empty leaves and projecting output
labels gives the lower bounds for leaves and depth. For the upper bounds,
run one optimal factor tree and then, at each leaf, run the other factor
tree. Coordinate inverse images of open sets are open, the depths add,
and the leaf counts multiply. The height statement follows from
\cref{cw:thm:product,cw:thm:minheight}; iterate the rank formula for $n$ factors.
\end{proof}

The rank formula is exact even when the two layer optima provide only rough
bounds on the product optimum. One can compute the exact product antichain
from \eqref{cw:eq:productsignature} by enumerating reduced pair words through
length $r+s-1$, testing their two projections, and retaining maximal words.
This is a finite algorithm; no assertion of polynomial output size is made.

Together with \cref{cw:prop:sums}, these results give a concrete algebra of
finite-depth behaviors. Sums take unions of downsets, equivalently intersections
of admissibility languages. Independent products take the reduced-word
languages whose coordinate projections belong to the factor signatures.
The relevant product operation is asynchronous interleaving of color changes,
not mere concatenation of the factor words.

\begin{writenoted}[Part~I's product law]
The rank law \eqref{cw:eq:productrank} is the coloring analogue of
Part~I's \cref{lem:product}, which the manuscript does not cite: for
Hausdorff spaces of last derivative ranks $h$ and $k$, that is, of
heights $h+1$ and $k+1$ in the convention of \cref{cw:sec:height}, the
product has last rank $h+k$, height $(h+1)+(k+1)-1$. By
\cref{cw:thm:minheight}, \eqref{cw:eq:productrank} says the same of the
least heights of Hausdorff representatives of the word profiles.
\end{writenoted}

\section{Higher-order information is indispensable}
\label{cw:sec:example}

The finite-derived-set theory makes pairwise accumulation graphs natural.
One might hope to extend it using derivative ranks, derivative cardinalities,
and a bounded number of local word statistics. The following construction
shows that progressively longer nested closures can change the exact query
budget while all shorter occurrence tests remain unchanged.

\begin{theorem}[Same space, same triple data, different query depth]
\label{cw:thm:separationexample}
There is a compact countable metrizable Hausdorff space $Z$ of
Cantor--Bendixson height four with two three-colorings $c_{\rm hard}$ and
$c_{\rm soft}$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Their closure signatures agree on every reduced word of length at most
three.
\item For each derivative index $j$ and color $a$, the sets
$Z^{(j)}\cap c_{\rm hard}^{-1}(a)$ and
$Z^{(j)}\cap c_{\rm soft}^{-1}(a)$ have the same cardinality.
\item Nevertheless,
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:examplevalues}
 \layers(Z,c_{\rm hard})=9,\quad \depth(Z,c_{\rm hard})=4;
 \qquad
 \layers(Z,c_{\rm soft})=7,\quad \depth(Z,c_{\rm soft})=3.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $\Sigma=\{0,1,2\}$. There are $3\cdot2^3=24$ reduced words
$a_1a_2a_3a_4$ of length four. Let $Z$ be the disjoint union of 24 copies
of $\Tw_4$, one indexed by each of these words.

On the component indexed by $a_1a_2a_3a_4$, use that same rank word for
$c_{\rm hard}$. Its component signature is the reduced-subsequence downset
of that word, by \cref{cw:lem:tower,cw:thm:recovery}. The total signature is
therefore all reduced words of length at most four.

On the same component, use the rank word $a_1a_1a_2a_3$ for
$c_{\rm soft}$. Its reduction is $a_1a_2a_3$. Every reduced three-letter
word appears as such a prefix, so the total soft signature is exactly all
reduced words of length at most three. This proves (i). By
\cref{cw:lem:universal}, their SCS lengths are respectively $2\cdot4+1=9$
and $2\cdot3+1=7$, giving (iii) by \cref{cw:thm:normal}.

Both colorings are defined on the identical underlying space. At derivative
three there are 24 roots, eight of each color for each coloring. At each
of derivatives zero, one and two, every color occurs countably infinitely
many times in both colorings: for the hard coloring the relevant level
colors range over the three possibilities as components vary; for the soft
coloring even the next-to-root level already repeats each component's root
color infinitely often. All spaces concerned are countable. Derivative four
and later are empty. Thus the exact common table is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
Derivative & Color 0 & Color 1 & Color 2\\
\midrule
$Z^{(0)},Z^{(1)},Z^{(2)}$ & $\aleph_0$ & $\aleph_0$ & $\aleph_0$\\
$Z^{(3)}$ & 8 & 8 & 8\\
$Z^{(j)}$, $j\ge4$ & 0 & 0 & 0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
This proves (ii).
\end{proof}

The two optimal master words may be taken as $012012012$ and $0120120$.
These are nine and seven layers, respectively; they are not nine and seven
questions. Balanced suffix search gives the four- and three-question trees.
The comparison preserves Boolean occurrence/nonoccurrence of short patterns,
not the actual subsets $F_v\subseteq Z$ or all possible local data. It should
not be read as an assertion that every conceivable local invariant fails.

\begin{proposition}[An unbounded hierarchy of missing information]
\label{cw:prop:hierarchy}
For every $r\ge2$, a common compact countable metrizable space of height $r$
has two three-colorings with signatures consisting respectively of all
reduced words of lengths at most $r$ and at most $r-1$.
Their layer counts are $2r+1$ and $2r-1$.
For $r=2^{d-1}$, $d\ge2$, their query depths are respectively $d+1$ and $d$,
although all occurrence tests of length at most $r-1$ agree.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Repeat the preceding construction on copies of $\Tw_r$ indexed by all reduced
$r$-letter words. Use the hard rank word $a_1\cdots a_r$ and the soft rank
word $a_1a_1a_2\cdots a_{r-1}$. Their reduced component words are all
length-$r$ words and all length-$(r-1)$ words, respectively. Apply the tower
profile, sum law and universal-word formula. If $r=2^{d-1}$, the two lengths
are $2^d+1$ and $2^d-1$, which straddle the power-of-two query threshold.
\end{proof}

Thus no fixed truncation order captures all finite-depth colored spaces.
The positive truncation theorem is necessarily tied to the proposed word
budget, and the graph formula is confined to the appropriate derivative
or signature-rank regime.

\section{Algorithms, certificates, and the effective boundary}
\label{cw:sec:algorithms}

The mathematical reduction separates two tasks: discovering a topological
signature, and solving the resulting finite word problem. Only the second
is automatically effective from a finite list of words. This distinction
prevents a hidden assumption that arbitrary topological closure or emptiness
is computable.

\subsection{Extracting a bounded signature}

Given operations for closure, intersection with each $A_a$, and deciding
emptiness of the resulting closed sets, one can compute all $F_v$ for
reduced words of length at most $m$ recursively. There are at most
$N(q,m)=q\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(q-1)^j$ such words. Store a word precisely when
its computed closed set is nonempty, then remove words that are proper
subsequences of other stored words. The result is the maximal antichain of
the truncated signature.

This is an algorithm \emph{relative to the stated operations}. An arbitrary
topological presentation need not supply them effectively. For a finite
poset, closure is downward reachability and the test is finite and explicit.
For a space of known Hausdorff height $h+1$, the derivative bound supplies the
complete cutoff $m=h+1$. For a general finite-depth space, one needs a rank
bound or some independent termination argument; finite depth alone is not
being offered as an effective stopping certificate from unspecified input.

\subsection{Exact SCS by progress-state search}

Let the finite basis be $B=\{b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(t)}\}$, with lengths
$\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_t$. A state
\[
 s=(s_1,\ldots,s_t),\qquad 0\le s_i\le\ell_i,
\]
records how many letters of each word have been matched greedily.
Appending a letter $a$ updates all coordinates simultaneously:
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:transition}
 s_i\longmapsto s_i+
 \mathbf 1\{s_i<\ell_i\text{ and }b^{(i)}_{s_i+1}=a\}.
\end{equation}
Start at the zero state. Breadth-first search reaches the terminal state
$(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_t)$ along a shortest master word. A predecessor map
reconstructs it. There are at most
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:states}
 \prod_{i=1}^t(\ell_i+1)
\end{equation}
states and $q$ outgoing transitions per state; straightforward transition
calculation takes $O(t)$ time. Thus an explicit worst-case bound is
$O(qt\prod_i(\ell_i+1))$, with comparable finite storage after accounting
for the stored state vectors.

For fixed alphabet size and signature rank, \cref{cw:prop:basisbound} bounds
this state space solely in terms of those two parameters. This is a
parameter-bound combinatorial algorithm \emph{after} obtaining the signature;
it is not a polynomial-time algorithm for arbitrary topological input.
In particular, it does not contradict the established three-letter SCS
hardness \cite{LT} or its repository applications.

\subsection{From a word to actual open questions}

Once an admissible word $w$ has been found, compute its elimination chain
$G_0\supseteq\cdots\supseteq G_m=\varnothing$.
The proof of \cref{cw:prop:elimination} gives explicit layers and suffixes:
\begin{equation}\label{cw:eq:questions}
 \lambda^{-1}(i)=G_{m-i}\setminus G_{m-i+1},\qquad
 U_j=X\setminus G_{m-j+1}.
\end{equation}
Binary search in these suffixes is a concrete optimal-depth strategy when
$w$ is shortest. The generator of the word may be untrusted: an independent
checker can verify its subsequence certificates against a known complete
basis, or check the elimination chain directly.

A failed proposed word has a particularly small semantic certificate:
a reduced $v$ with $\len v\le\len w$, $F_v\ne\varnothing$, and
$v\not\preceq w$. The combinatorial part is easy to verify. The topological
nonemptiness still requires valid evidence in the presentation at hand.
On a finite poset, a colored chain supplies it. On a Hausdorff accumulation
space, a list of finitely many isolated sample points generally does not.

\subsection{What would be formalized}

The proof naturally divides into three interfaces. First, finite words,
run reduction, greedy subsequences, diagonal avoidance, and the separator
construction are purely combinatorial. Second, the lattice of closed sets
supplies monotone contractive idempotent operators and finite unions; this
proves expansion and duality. Third, Hausdorff derivatives and compact tower
constructions establish the topological realization theorems.

A Lean development can keep search untrusted and verify certificates against
these interfaces. The finite-budget theorem can be stated with integer bounds
$m\le2^d$ instead of real logarithms. The present package supplies executable
finite checks but no Lean proof files and no assertion that these interfaces
have been kernel-checked.

\section{Independent verification and reproducibility}
\label{cw:sec:verification}

The program \path{code/04-closure-words-verify.py} uses only the Python
standard library. The recorded results are in
\path{data/04-closure-words-verification.json}.
They come from an actual completed run under Python 3.13.5. Assertions are verification conditions,
so the program explicitly refuses execution with optimization flag
\texttt{-O}.

\subsection{Exhaustive finite topologies}

Every finite topology is the upper-set topology of its specialization
preorder. The program enumerates all reflexive transitive relations on each
labeled set of size at most four, including non-$T_0$ spaces, and all
three-colorings. For each one it calculates open sets and closure directly
from the relation.

Two independent computations are then compared. The first is minimax over
all open questions, with no use of closure words or subsequences. For a
nonempty current state $S$, it uses
\[
 d(S)=\begin{cases}
 0,&S\text{ monochromatic},\\
 1+\min_U\max\{d(S\cap U),d(S\setminus U)\},&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
\]
where only nontrivial open splits are allowed and the minimum over no splits
is infinite. It separately minimizes leaf count by adding the two branch
leaf counts. The second computation builds the closure signature, extracts
its antichain, and performs progress-state SCS search.

A finite preorder has a finite tree for a given coloring precisely when the
color is constant on each topological indistinguishability class. If a class
contains two colors, those colors give arbitrarily long alternating detected
words and cannot be separated by open sets. Otherwise the coloring descends
to the finite quotient poset, whose chains have at most $n$ points. This
justifies the program's finite-signature cutoff at $n$, with a check at
$n+1$ for the excluded case.

The enumerated counts are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
Points & Labeled topologies & Three-colorings & Finite-tree colorings\\
\midrule
1 & 1 & 3 & 3\\
2 & 4 & 36 & 30\\
3 & 29 & 783 & 597\\
4 & 355 & 28,755 & 21,009\\
\midrule
Total & 389 & 29,577 & 21,639\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These count labeled topologies, not homeomorphism classes. Every topology
on at most four points is covered, with possible isomorphic repetitions.

For every coloring, the program also tests every candidate word of lengths
one through the number of points, including nonreduced candidates. It
compares greedy closed-set elimination, the full signature constraint test,
and the short-certificate test. The recorded totals are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule
Check & Recorded count\\
\midrule
Candidate-word admissibility comparisons & 3,481,578\\
Rejected candidates with short failure certificates & 2,847,462\\
Distinct finite signatures encountered & 109\\
Constructed separating-word tests & 7,296\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All comparisons and assertions passed.

\subsection{Product, graph, and extremal checks}

For all 16 finite-depth two-colorings of labeled topologies on one or two
points, the program checks all 256 ordered products. It constructs the
product preorder independently and compares the actual nested closed sets
with the rectangular identity \eqref{cw:eq:rectangle}. The record contains
9,856 such set-identity tests; all passed, as did the rank and product
leaf/depth bounds.

It separately enumerates all 4,165 loopless digraphs on labeled vertex sets
of sizes one through four. A subset-based acyclicity calculation obtains
$\tau(G)$, and progress-state SCS independently obtains the optimum for
singleton and arc constraints. All tests agree with $\layers=q+\tau(G)$.
These are graph checks, not a finite sampling of arbitrary infinite
rank-one spaces.

For the universal-word constant, the verified parameter ranges are
$q=2$, $1\le r\le8$; $q=3$, $1\le r\le6$; and $q=4$,
$1\le r\le3$. Each cyclic upper-bound word is checked against every
length-$r$ reduced word. Every reduced word one letter shorter than the
claimed optimum is independently rejected by some constraint.
The 24 hard and 24 soft rank-word assignments in
\cref{cw:thm:separationexample} are recorded explicitly; their reduced maximal
bases have sizes 24 and 12.

\subsection{Reproduction and limits of the evidence}

From this report's directory, run
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 code/04-closure-words-verify.py --output <scratch>/rerun.json
\end{lstlisting}
where \texttt{<scratch>} is a directory outside the report.
This keeps the delivered verification record unchanged. The default
\texttt{--max-n 4 --q 3} regenerates all counts quoted above.
The measured complete run took 4.282 seconds in the execution environment;
this is not a portable performance guarantee. The elapsed field is expected
to vary between runs.

To compile the article, run \texttt{make pdf}; it invokes \texttt{pdflatex}
three times, without an external bibliography processor. The article does
not require executing the verifier, retrieving web content, or using an
external graphics file.

The checks establish the stated finite computational comparisons only.
Universal validity, compactness, Hausdorff derivative descent, and the
infinite-space counterexamples rest on the written proofs. The tests do not
establish historical novelty, publication acceptance, or formal verification.

\begin{writenoted}[Shipped names]
The delivered text reads ``The program \texttt{code/verify.py}'', ``The
recorded results are in \texttt{results/verification.json}'' and ``From
the package root, run \texttt{python3 code/verify.py -{}-output
results/rerun.json}''; here \path{code/verify.py} is Part~I's verifier,
so the command above uses the shipped name. Without \texttt{-{}-output}
the shipped verifier writes \path{results/verification.json} beside

the recorded file is \path{data/04-closure-words-verification.json}, and
the digests in \path{data/04-closure-words-validation.json} describe the
delivered manuscript, PDF and verifier, not this merged report. The
